ℍ 1 \mathbb{H}^{1} -Random attractors for 2D stochastic convective Brinkman-Forchheimer equations in unbounded domains 1,2 Department of Mathematics, Indian Institute of Technology Roorkee-IIT Roorkee, Haridwar Highway, Roorkee, Uttarakhand 247667, INDIA. e-mail: Manil T. Mohan: maniltmohan@ma.iitr.ac.in, maniltmohan@gmail.com. e-mail: Kush Kinra: kkinra@ma.iitr.ac.in. * Corresponding author. Key words: Stochastic convective Brinkman-Forchheimer equations, unbounded domains, cylindrical Wiener process, random dynamical system, absorbing sets, asymptotically compactness, random attractors. Mathematics Subject Classification (2020): Primary 35B41, 35Q35; Secondary 37L55, 37N10, 35R60.
Kush Kinra 1 and Manil T. Mohan 2*

Abstract
. The asymptotic behavior of solutions of two dimensional stochastic convective Brinkman-Forchheimer (2D SCBF) equations in unbounded domains is discussed in this work (for example, Poincaré domains). We first prove the existence of ℍ 1 \mathbb{H}^{1} -random attractors for the stochastic flow generated by 2D SCBF equations (for the absorption exponent r ∈ [ 1 , 3 ] r\in[1,3] ) perturbed by an additive noise on Poincaré domains. Furthermore, we deduce the existence of a unique invariant measure in ℍ 1 \mathbb{H}^{1} for the 2D SCBF equations defined on Poincaré domains. In addition, a remark on the extension of these results to general unbounded domains is also discussed. Finally, for 2D SCBF equations forced by additive one-dimensional Wiener noise, we prove the upper semicontinuity of the random attractors, when the domain changes from bounded to unbounded (Poincaré).



1.
Introduction

The analysis of long time behavior of infinite dimensional dynamical systems including two-dimensional Navier-Stokes equations (2D NSE) is well investigated in
[
55
]
. The existence of a global attractor for 2D NSE on some unbounded domains like Poincaré domains is obtained in
[
52
]
. Following the work
[
52
]
, the existence of global attractors for the two-dimensional convective Brinkman-Forchheimer (CBF) equations in
\mathbb{H},
for the absorption exponent
r\in[1,\infty)
and the external forcing
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
(from the Gelfand triple
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{H}\subset\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
, see section
2
for functional framework) in Poincaré like domain is proved in
[
47
]
. The upper semicontinuity of global attractor with respect to domain, that is, when domain changes from bounded to unbounded (Poincaré) domain is also established in
[
47
]
. In
[
33
]
, the author extended the work
[
52
]
and showed that if the external forcing term is in the natural space
\mathbb{H}
, then the global attractor for 2D NSE is compact not only in the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
-norm but also in the
\mathbb{H}^{1}
-norm, and it attracts all bounded sets in
\mathbb{H}
in the metric of
\mathbb{V}
. Whenever the forcing
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{H}
, the existence of
\mathbb{H}^{1}
-global attractors for 2D CBF equations in unbounded domains for the absorption exponent
r\in[1,3]
is proved in
[
49
]
. It is well-investigated in the literature that a large class of stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs) generate random dynamical systems (RDS) (cf.
[
2
]
). The analysis of infinite dimensional RDS is also an essential branch in the study of qualitative properties of SPDEs (see
[
6
,
16
,
21
]
, etc for more details). The random dynamics of 2D stochastic Navier-Stokes equations (SNSE) has been carried out in the works
[
6
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
19
,
20
,
21
]
, etc and the references therein.

In this work, we are concerned about the long time behavior of 2D SCBF equations in Poincaré domains
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and general unbounded domains. Let
\mathcal{O}
satisfy the following assumption:

Assumption 1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be an open, connected and may be unbounded subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the boundary of which is uniformly of class
\mathrm{C}^{3}
(cf.
[
30
]
). For the domain
\mathcal{O}
, we also assume that, there exists a positive constant
\lambda_{1}
such that the following Poincaré inequality is satisfied:

(1.1)

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\int_{\mathcal{O}}|\psi(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x\leq\int_{\mathcal{O}}|\nabla\psi(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x,\ \text{ for all }\ \psi\in\mathbb{H}^{1}_{0}(\mathcal{O}).

A domain in which Poincaré inequality is satisfied, we call it as a Poincaré domain and if
\mathcal{O}
is bounded in some direction, then the Poincaré inequality (
1.1
) holds. For example, one can consider
\mathcal{O}=\mathbb{R}\times(-L,L),

L>0
(see page 306,
[
55
]
).

The convective Brinkman-Forchheimer (CBF) equations characterize the motion of incompressible viscous fluid through a rigid, homogeneous, isotropic, porous medium. The 2D SCBF equations perturbed by additive noise are given by

(1.2)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{u}&=[\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{u}-(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}-\alpha\boldsymbol{u}-\beta|\boldsymbol{u}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{u}-\nabla p+\boldsymbol{f}]\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathrm{W}(t),\text{ in }\ \mathcal{O}\times(0,\infty),\\
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}&=0,\ \text{ in }\ \mathcal{O}\times(0,\infty),\\
\boldsymbol{u}&=\boldsymbol{0},\ \ \text{ on }\ \partial\mathcal{O}\times[0,\infty),\\
\boldsymbol{u}(0)&=\boldsymbol{x},\ \text{ in }\ \mathcal{O},\end{aligned}}}\right.

where
\mathrm{W}(\cdot)
is a given Hilbert space valued Wiener process on some given filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\{\mathscr{F}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}},\mathbb{P})
whose properties will be specified in section
2
. Here
\boldsymbol{u}(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
p(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}
and
\boldsymbol{f}(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
represent the velocity field, the pressure field and an external forcing, respectively, at position
x
and time
t
. The constant
\mu>0
represents the Brinkman coefficient (effective viscosity), the positive constants
\alpha
and
\beta
represent the Darcy (permeability of porous medium) and Forchheimer coefficients, respectively. The exponent
r\in[1,\infty)
is called the absorption exponent and
r=3
is known as the critical exponent. The exponents
r=1,2,3
are physically relevant and they represent linear, quadratic and cubic growths, receptively. For
\alpha=\beta=0
, we obtain the classical 2D stochastic Navier-Stokes equations, and one can consider the system (
1.2
) as a damped SNSE with the nonlinear damping
\alpha\boldsymbol{u}+\beta|\boldsymbol{u}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{u}
. For the unique solvability of the deterministic two- and three-dimensional CBF equations in bounded and periodic domains, the interested readers are referred to see
[
3
,
26
,
31
,
34
,
45
]
, etc and for its stochastic counterpart, see
[
46
]
.

In most of the results available in the literature, the existence of random attractors is heavily based on random compact attracting set (using compact embedding). But in this work, we are not able to find random compact attracting set due to following two reasons:

(i)

For the stochastic 2D NSE and related models, for
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{H}
, the existence of random absorbing sets in more regular spaces than
\mathbb{V}
is not available (for example in
\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}^{s})
, for
s>\frac{1}{2}
).

(ii)

The domain
\mathcal{O}
is unbounded.

In the deterministic case, the above mentioned difficulties were resolved by different methods, cf.
[
1
,
29
,
52
]
, etc for the autonomous case and
[
14
,
15
]
, etc for the non-autonomous case. For SPDEs, the methods available in the deterministic case have also been generalized by several authors (see for example,
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
58
]
, etc). The concept of an asymptotically compact cocycle was introduced in
[
14
]
and the authors proved the existence of attractors fornon-autonomous 2D NSE. Later, several authors used this method to prove the existence of random attractors in unbounded domains, see for example
[
9
,
12
,
13
,
27
,
35
,
36
,
38
,
47
,
49
,
53
,
54
,
58
]
etc. The existence of a unique random attractor for the 2D and 3D SCBF equations (
1.2
) perturbed by additive rough noise in
\mathbb{H}
is proved in
[
38
]
.

Recently, for
r\in[1,3]
, the existence of random attractors in
\mathbb{H}
and
\mathbb{V}
for the stochastic flow generated by 2D SCBF equations in bounded domains perturbed by small additive smooth noise is obtained in
[
39
]
. The authors in
[
39
]
also proved the upper semicontinuity of the random attractor, when the noise term goes to zero. In this work, first we prove the existence of random attractors for 2D SCBF equations for
r\in[1,3]
in
\mathbb{V}
on unbounded (Poincaré) domains. We use the concepts developed in
[
10
]
to prove the desired results for the existence of random attractors. In
[
21
]
, authors proved that the existence of compact invariant random set is a sufficient condition for the existence of invariant measures. Since, random attractor itself is a compact invariant set, the existence of invariant measures is confirmed. In addition, we prove the uniqueness of invariant measure for our model.

Next, we prove the upper semicontinuity of the random attractors with respect to domain, that is, when domain changes from bounded to unbounded (Poincaré) domain. Due to some technical difficulty (related to the RKHS of Wiener process and the cut-off problem), we are not able to prove the upper semicontinuity of random attractors with respect to domain for the system (
1.2
). But, we are able to prove if the noise in (
1.2
) replaced by a finite dimensional noise (see (
6.1
) below). The existence of a unique random attractor for 2D SCBF equations perturbed by finite dimensional noise is proved in
[
40
]
(see Section 5,
[
40
]
). Upper semicontinuity of global attractors with respect to domain for some physically relevant deterministic models such as Klein-Gordon-Schrödinger equation, 2D CBF equations and 2D NSE is established in
[
44
]
,
[
47
]
and
[
59
]
, respectively. We extend the same idea to prove the upper semicontinuity of random attractors with respect to domain for 2D SCBF equations.

In the literature, the concept of upper semicontinuity of random attractors with respect to domain was introduced in
[
41
]
and the authors proved upper semicontinuity of random attractors with respect to domain for stochastic FitzHugh-Nagumo equations (SFNE). Later, using this concept, upper semicontinuity of random attractors with respect to domain for g-Navier-Stokes equations (g-NSE) was proved in
[
42
]
. While using the concept introduced in
[
41
]
to the models SFNE and g-NSE, the authors have not taken care of the boundary conditions, while proving the strong convergence of the solutions on the sequence of bounded domains to the solution on the unbounded domain, which is a crucial step in the theory. Due to this reason, it appears to us that the method developed in the works
[
41
,
42
]
, etc have serious flaws, and we have not used the theory established in
[
41
]
. But, one can prove the upper semicontinuity of random attractors with respect to domain for SFNE and g-NSE using the same method which we apply for 2D SCBF equation in Section
6
.

Let us now explain the difficulty for extending the results obtained in this work for the case
r>3
. The following equality

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{O}}(-\Delta\boldsymbol{v}(x))\cdot|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v}(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x

(1.3)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{O}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{2}|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{r-1}\/\mathrm{d}\/x+4\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{r-1}{(r+1)^{2}}}}\right]\int_{\mathcal{O}}|\nabla|\boldsymbol{v}(x)|^{\frac{r+1}{2}}|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x

is useful in the whole space or periodic domains. But, we are not able to use the above equality in bounded domains as
\mathcal{P}(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{u})
need not be zero on the boundary, and
\mathcal{P}
and
-\Delta
are not necessarily commuting (for a counterexample, see Example 2.19,
[
51
]
). In order to prove the existence of random attractors for 2D SCBF equations in
\mathbb{V}
, we do not use the above equality for
r\in[1,3]
. Due to this difficulty, it appears to us that the results obtained in the works
[
32
,
57
]
(random exponential attractors and random attractors for the 3D damped NSE) hold true in periodic domains only.

Aims of the work:
The major aims and novelties of this work are:

(i)

Existence of a unique random attractor for the system (
1.2
) with
r\in[1,3]
on unbounded Poincaré domains in
\mathbb{V}
.

(ii)

Existence of a unique invariant measure for the system (
1.2
) in
\mathbb{V}
.

(iii)

Upper semicontinuity of the random attractors with respect to domain, that is, when domain changes from bounded to unbounded (Poincaré) domain.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In section
2
, we first define the function spaces, which are needed in this work. Then, we define the linear and nonlinear operators and discuss their properties. Abstract formulation of the 2D SCBF equations (
1.2
), the assumption on the Reproducing Kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) and the solvability results for the 2D SCBF equations are also provided in the same section. In section
3
, we define metric dynamical system and random dynamical system for our model. Section
4
is devoted for establishing the existence of random attractors for the 2D SCBF equations for
r\in[1,3]
in
\mathbb{V}
by proving the asymptotically compactness property of RDS
\varphi
in
\mathbb{V}
(Theorem
4.7
). In section
5
, we establish the existence of a unique invariant measure the 2D SCBF equations (
1.2
) in
\mathbb{V}
(Theorem
5.3
). In the final section, we first find the random absorbing set (Lemmas
6.2
and
6.3
) and uniform tail estimates (Lemmas
6.4
and
6.5
) for the solution of 2D SCBF equations. Then, we prove the upper semicontinuity of the random attractor, when domain changes from bounded to unbounded (Theorem
6.9
).

2.
Mathematical Formulation

In this section, we provide the necessary function spaces needed to obtain the existence of random attractors for the 2D SCBF equations (
1.2
).

2.1.
Function spaces

Let
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and satisfies Assumption
1.1
(may be bounded or unbounded). We define the space

\mathcal{V}:=\{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{R}^{2}):\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=0\},

where
\mathrm{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O};\mathbb{R}^{2})
denote the space of all infinitely differentiable functions (
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-valued) with compact support in
\mathcal{O}
. Let
\mathbb{H}
,
\mathbb{V}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}
denote the completion of
\mathcal{V}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathbb{R}^{2}):=\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O})
,
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathcal{O};\mathbb{R}^{2}):=\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathcal{O})
and
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathcal{O};\mathbb{R}^{2}):=\mathbb{L}^{p}(\mathcal{O})
(
2<p<\infty
) norms, respectively. The space
\mathbb{H}
is endowed with the norm
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}:=\int_{\mathcal{O}}|\boldsymbol{u}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x,
the norm on the space
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}
is represented by
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}}^{2}:=\int_{\mathcal{O}}|\boldsymbol{u}(x)|^{p}\/\mathrm{d}\/x,
for
2<p<\infty
and the norm on the space
\mathbb{V}
is given by
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}=\int_{\mathcal{O}}|\boldsymbol{u}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x+\int_{\mathcal{O}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x.
Using the Poincaré inequality (
1.1
), one can easily see that the norm
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}
is equivalent to the norm
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}:=\int_{\mathcal{O}}|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(x)|^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/x.
The inner product in the Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
is denoted by
(\cdot,\cdot),
in the Hilbert space
\mathbb{V}
is denoted by
(\!(\cdot,\cdot)\!)
and the induced duality, for instance between the spaces
\mathbb{V}
and
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
, and
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}
and its dual
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\frac{p}{p-1}}
is denoted by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle.
It should be noted that
\mathbb{V}
is densely and continuously embedded into
\mathbb{H}
and
\mathbb{H}
can be identified with its dual
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
and we have the
Gelfand triple
:
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{H}\cong\mathbb{H}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
. In the rest of the paper, we use the notation
\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O}):=\mathrm{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathbb{R}^{2})
for the second order Sobolev spaces.

2.2.
Linear operator

Let
\mathcal{P}:\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O})\to\mathbb{H}
denote the Helmholtz-Hodge orthogonal projection (cf.
[
43
]
). Let us define the Stokes operator

\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{u}:=-\mathcal{P}\Delta\boldsymbol{u},\;\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}).

The operator
\mathrm{A}
is a linear continuous operator from
\mathbb{V}
into
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
, satisfying

\langle\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\rangle=(\!(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\!),\ \ \ \boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{V}.

Since the boundary of
\mathcal{O}
is uniformly of class
\mathrm{C}^{3}
, this infer that
\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})=\mathbb{V}\cap\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O})
and
\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\mathbb{H}}
defines a norm in
\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}),
which is equivalent to the one in
\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O})
(cf. Lemma 1,
[
30
]
). Above argument implies that
\mathcal{P}:\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O})\to\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O})
is a bounded operator. Note that the operator
\mathrm{A}
is a non-negative self-adjoint operator in
\mathbb{H}
and

(2.1)

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u}\rangle=\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2},\ \textrm{ for all }\ \boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{V},\ \text{ so that }\ \|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\mathbb{V}}.

2.3.
Bilinear operator

Let us define the
trilinear form

b(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{R}
by

b(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})=\int_{\mathcal{O}}(\boldsymbol{u}(x)\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}(x)\cdot\boldsymbol{w}(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x=\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}\int_{\mathcal{O}}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}(x)\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{v}_{j}(x)}{\partial x_{i}}\boldsymbol{w}_{j}(x)\/\mathrm{d}\/x.

If
\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}
are such that the linear map
b(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v},\cdot)
is continuous on
\mathbb{V}
, the corresponding element of
\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
is denoted by
\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})
. We represent
\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{u})=\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u})=\mathcal{P}[(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}]
. Using an integration by parts, one can easily see that

(2.2)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} b(\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{2},\boldsymbol{u}_{3})&=-b(\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{3},\boldsymbol{u}_{2}),\ \text{ for all }\ \boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{2},\boldsymbol{u}_{3}\in\mathbb{V},\\
b(\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{2},\boldsymbol{u}_{2})&=0,\ \text{ for all }\ \boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\in\mathbb{V}.\end{aligned}}}\right.

Remark 2.1
.

The following inequalities are used frequently in the sequel.

(i)

Using Ladyzhenskaya’s inequality (Lemma 1, Chapter I,
[
43
]
), we find

(2.3)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}(\mathcal{O})}\leq 2^{1/4}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O})}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O})},\ \ \ \boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{H}^{1,2}_{0}(\mathcal{O}).

(ii)

The following estimates on the trilinear form
b(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
(see Chapter 2, section 2.3,
[
56
]
):

\bullet

\text{for all }\ \boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in\mathbb{V},\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}),\boldsymbol{u}_{3}\in\mathbb{H}
, we have

(2.4)

\displaystyle|b(\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{2},\boldsymbol{u}_{3})|

\displaystyle\leq C\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{V}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{V}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{3}\|_{\mathbb{H}},

\bullet

\text{for all }\ \boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}),\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\in\mathbb{V},\boldsymbol{u}_{3}\in\mathbb{H}
, we get

(2.5)

\displaystyle|b(\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{2},\boldsymbol{u}_{3})|

\displaystyle\leq C\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\|_{\mathbb{V}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{3}\|_{\mathbb{H}}.

Remark 2.2
.

If
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})),
then
\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{u})\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}).
Indeed, by (
2.4
), we have

(2.6)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{u}(t))\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|^{3}_{\mathbb{V}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t\leq CT^{1/2}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|^{3}_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))},

which is finite.

Lemma 2.3

(Lemma 5.2,
[
11
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{1}\subset\mathcal{O}
, which is bounded, and
\psi:[0,T]\times\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a
\mathrm{C}^{1}
-class function such that
\mathrm{supp}\ \psi(t,\cdot)\subset\mathcal{O}_{1},
for
t\in[0,T],
and

\sup_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}\sup_{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{1}}|D_{i}\psi^{j}(t,x)|=C<\infty.

Let
\boldsymbol{v}_{m}
converges to
\boldsymbol{v}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
weakly and in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{1}))
strongly. Then, we have

\int_{0}^{T}b(\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(t),\psi(t))\/\mathrm{d}\/t\to\int_{0}^{T}b(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t),\psi(t))\/\mathrm{d}\/t\ \text{ as }\ m\to\infty.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a bounded sequence in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V}),
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
be such that

(2.7)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\xrightharpoonup{w}\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})),

(2.8)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V}).

Then for any
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))
, we have

(2.9)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}b\big(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\to\int_{0}^{T}b\big(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t,

(2.10)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}b\big(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t),\boldsymbol{w}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\to\int_{0}^{T}b\big(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{w}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t,

(2.11)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}b\big(\boldsymbol{w}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\to\int_{0}^{T}b\big(\boldsymbol{w}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t,

as
n\to\infty
, for all
T>0.

Proof.

We shall prove this Lemma in three steps.

Step I.

Proof of (
2.9
):
Let us first consider

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{T}b\big(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t-\int_{0}^{T}b\big(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\big|b\big(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\big)\big|\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\int_{0}^{T}\big|b\big(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\big)\big|\/\mathrm{d}\/t

(2.12)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\big|b\big(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t))\big)\big|\/\mathrm{d}\/t=:K_{1}+K_{2}+K_{3}.

Using (
2.4
), Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we estimate
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
as

\displaystyle K_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{V}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{V}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|^{3/2}_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})}}}\right]\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}-\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\|^{3/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))}

(2.13)

\displaystyle\to 0\ \text{ as }\ n\to\infty,

\displaystyle K_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{V}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{V}}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t))\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq C\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}-\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}-\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}-\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))}

(2.14)

\displaystyle\to 0\text{ as }n\to\infty.

By Remark
2.2
, we know that
\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}).
Hence, using the weak convergence in the space
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))
(see (
2.7
)), we get

(2.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}K_{3}\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\big|\big(\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t))\big)\big|\/\mathrm{d}\/t=0.

Combining (
2.13
)-(
2.15
) and using in (
2.3
), we obtain (
2.9
).

Step II.

Proof of (
2.10
):
Next, we consider

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{T}b\big(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t),\boldsymbol{w}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t-\int_{0}^{T}b\big(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{w}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\big|b\big(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{w}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\big)\big|\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\int_{0}^{T}\big|b\big(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{w}(t),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t))\big)\big|\/\mathrm{d}\/t

(2.16)

\displaystyle=:K_{4}+K_{5}.

Using (
2.4
), Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we estimate
K_{4}
as

\displaystyle K_{4}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{V}}\|\boldsymbol{w}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{V}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{w}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg[\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})}\bigg]\|\boldsymbol{w}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}-\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\|\boldsymbol{w}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))}

(2.17)

\displaystyle\to 0\ \text{ as }\ n\to\infty.

Making use of (
2.4
), one can show that
\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}),
since

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{w}(t))\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}\|\boldsymbol{w}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{w}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq CT^{1/2}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\|\boldsymbol{w}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\|\boldsymbol{w}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))}<\infty.

Hence, using the weak convergence given in (
2.7
), we get

(2.18)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}K_{5}\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\big|\big(\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{w}(t)),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t))\big)\big|\/\mathrm{d}\/t=0.

Combining (
2.17
)-(
2.18
) and using it in (
2.3
), we obtain (
2.10
).

Step III.

Proof of (
2.11
):
Finally, we consider

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{T}b\big(\boldsymbol{w}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t-\int_{0}^{T}b\big(\boldsymbol{w}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\big|b\big(\boldsymbol{w}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\big)\big|\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\int_{0}^{T}\big|b\big(\boldsymbol{w}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t))\big)\big|\/\mathrm{d}\/t

(2.19)

\displaystyle=:K_{6}+K_{7}.

Once again using (
2.4
), Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we obtain

\displaystyle K_{6}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{w}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{w}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{V}}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{V}}\|\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t))\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq C\|\boldsymbol{w}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})}\|\boldsymbol{w}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}-\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}-\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\boldsymbol{w}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}-\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))}}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))}

(2.20)

\displaystyle\to 0\text{ as }n\to\infty.

One can prove that
\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}),
by using (
2.4
),
and hence, using the weak convergence in the space
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))
(see (
2.7
)), we get

(2.21)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}K_{7}\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\big|\big(\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{w}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t)),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t))\big)\big|\/\mathrm{d}\/t=0.

Combining (
2.20
)-(
2.21
) and using in (
2.3
), we finally obtain (
2.11
).
∎

2.4.
Nonlinear operator

Let us now consider the operator
\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{u}):=\mathcal{P}(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{u})
. It is immediate that
\langle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{u}),\boldsymbol{u}\rangle=\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}^{r+1}
and the map
\mathcal{C}(\cdot):\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}\to\mathbb{V}^{\prime}+\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}
. For all
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}
, the map is Gateaux differentiable with Gateaux derivative

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathcal{P}(\boldsymbol{v}),&\text{ for }r=1,\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathcal{P}(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v})+(r-1)\mathcal{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{u}}{|\boldsymbol{u}|^{3-r}}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})}}\right),&\text{ if }\boldsymbol{u}\neq\mathbf{0},\\
\mathbf{0},&\text{ if }\boldsymbol{u}=\mathbf{0},\end{array}}}\right.&\text{ for }1<r<3,\\
\mathcal{P}(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{v})+(r-1)\mathcal{P}(\boldsymbol{u}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{r-3}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})),&\text{ for }r\geq 3,\end{array}}}\right.

for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}
. For any
r\in[1,\infty)
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\in\mathbb{V}\cap\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}
, we have (see subsection 2.4,
[
46
]
)

(2.26)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1})-\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{u}_{2}),\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\rangle\geq 0.

We need the following convergence result in the sequel.

Lemma 2.5

(
[
38
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{1}\subset\mathcal{O}
, which is bounded, and
\psi:[0,T]\times\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a continuous function such that
\mathrm{supp}\ \psi(t,\cdot)\subset\mathcal{O}_{1},
for
t\in[0,T],
and

\sup_{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{1}}|\psi(t,x)|=C<\infty.

Assume that
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is a bounded sequence in the space
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}),\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}),

\boldsymbol{v}_{m}
converges to
\boldsymbol{v}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
weakly and
\boldsymbol{v}_{m}
converges to
\boldsymbol{v}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{1}))
strongly. Then for any
r\in[1,3]
with
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathrm{L}^{4}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
and for any
r>3
with
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
,

(2.27)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\big\langle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}_{m}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)),\psi(t)\big\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/t\to\int_{0}^{T}\big\langle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)),\psi(t)\big\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/t\ \text{ as }\ m\to\infty.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a bounded sequence in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
be such that (
2.7
)-(
2.8
) hold true.
Then for any
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))
and for any
r\geq 1
, we have

(2.28)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\big(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t\to\int_{0}^{T}\big(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t,

as
n\to\infty
, for all
T>0.

Proof.

Let us consider

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{T}\big(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t-\int_{0}^{T}\big(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\big|\big(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t))-\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\big)\big|\/\mathrm{d}\/t

(2.29)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\big|\big(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t))\big)\big|\/\mathrm{d}\/t=:\widetilde{K}_{1}+\widetilde{K}_{2}.

For
r=1
, it is immediate that
\widetilde{K}_{1}\to 0
as
n\to\infty.
For
r>1
, we estimate
\widetilde{K}_{1}
using Taylor’s formula (Theorem 7.9.1,
[
18
]
), Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev’s embedding as

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}\|\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\theta(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t))+(1-\theta)(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)))(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t))\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\theta\bigg)\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq r\int_{0}^{T}\bigg[\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)\|^{r-1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2r}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)\|^{r-1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2r}}\bigg]\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2r}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\bigg[\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)\|^{r-1}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)\|^{r-1}_{\mathbb{V}}\bigg]\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg[\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}+\boldsymbol{w}\|^{r-1}_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}+\|\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{w}\|^{r-1}_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\bigg]\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}-\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))}

(2.30)

\displaystyle\to 0\text{ as }n\to\infty.

Now, one can show that
\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{w})\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
, since

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t))\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)\|^{2r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2r}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)\|^{2r}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq CT\|\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{w}\|^{2r}_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}<\infty.

Hence, using the weak convergence (
2.7
) in the space
\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))
, we get

(2.31)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\widetilde{K}_{2}\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\big|\big(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{w}(t)),\mathrm{A}(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t))\big)\big|\/\mathrm{d}\/t=0.

Combining (
2.30
)-(
2.31
) and using it in (
2.4
), we finally deduce (
2.28
).
∎

2.5.
Stochastic convective Brinkman-Forchheimer equations

In this subsection, we provide an abstract formulation of the system (
1.2
), assumptions on the noise and discuss global solvability results.
On taking projection
\mathcal{P}
onto the first equation in (
1.2
), we obtain

(2.32)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{u}(t)+\{\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{u}(t)+\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{u}(t))+\alpha\boldsymbol{u}(t)+\beta\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{u}(t))\}\/\mathrm{d}\/t&=\boldsymbol{f}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathrm{W}(t),\ \ \ t\geq 0,\\
\boldsymbol{u}(0)&=\boldsymbol{x},\end{aligned}}}\right.

for
r\in[1,3]
, where
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{V},\ \boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{H}
and
\text{W}(t),\ t\in\mathbb{R},
is a two-sided cylindrical Wiener process in
\mathbb{H}
with its Reproducing Kernel Hilbert space (RKHS)
\mathrm{K}
satisfying the following assumption.

Assumption 2.7
.

\mathrm{K}\subset\mathbb{V}\cap\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O})
is a Hilbert space such that for some
\delta\in(0,1/2),

(2.33)

\displaystyle\mathrm{A}^{-\delta}:\mathrm{K}\to\mathbb{V}\cap\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O})\ \text{ is }\ \gamma\text{-radonifying.}

Remark 2.8
.

Since
\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})=\mathbb{V}\cap\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O})
, Assumption
2.7
can be reformulated in the following way also (see
[
10
]
).
\mathrm{K}
is a Hilbert space such that
\mathrm{K}\subset\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
and, for some
\delta\in(0,1/2)
, the map

(2.34)

\displaystyle\mathrm{A}^{-\delta-1}:\mathrm{K}\to\mathbb{H}\ \text{ is }\ \gamma\text{-radonifying.}

The condition (
2.34
) also says that the mapping
\mathrm{A}^{-\delta-1}:\mathrm{K}\to\mathbb{H}
is Hilbert-Schmidt. If
\mathcal{O}
is a bounded domain, then
\mathrm{A}^{-s}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{H}
is Hilbert-Schmidt if and only if
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}^{-2s}<\infty,
where
\mathrm{A}e_{j}=\lambda_{j}e_{j},j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\{e_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is an orthogonal basis of
\mathbb{H}
. In bounded domains, it is well known that
\lambda_{j}\sim j,
for large
j
(growth of eigenvalues) and hence
\mathrm{A}^{-s}
is Hilbert-Schmidt if and only if
s>\frac{1}{2}.
In other words, with
\mathrm{K}=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}^{s+1}),
the embedding
\mathrm{K}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{V}\cap\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O})
is
\gamma
-radonifying if and only if
s>\frac{1}{2}.
Thus, Assumption
2.7
is satisfied for any
\delta>0.
In fact, the condition (
2.33
) holds if and only if the operator
\mathrm{A}^{-(s+1+\delta)}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{V}\cap\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O})
is
\gamma
-radonifying. The requirement of
\delta<\frac{1}{2}
in Assumption
2.7
is necessary because we need (see subsection
3.1
) the corresponding Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process has to take values in
\mathbb{V}\cap\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O})
.

3.
RDS Generated by 2D SCBF Equations

In this section, we discuss the random dynamical system generated by the 2D SCBF equations (
2.32
).

3.1.
Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process

Let us first characterize Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes under Assumption
2.7
(for more details see section 3,
[
39
]
). Let us define
\mathrm{X}:=\mathbb{V}\cap\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O})
and let
\mathrm{E}
denote the completion of
\mathrm{A}^{-\delta}\mathrm{X}
with respect to the image norm
\|x\|_{\mathrm{E}}=\|\mathrm{A}^{-\delta}x\|_{\mathrm{X}},\ \text{for }\ x\in\mathrm{X},\text{where }\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{X}}=\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}.
For
\xi\in(0,1/2),
we define

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}^{\xi}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R},\mathrm{E})

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\omega\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R},\mathrm{E}):\omega(0)=0,\ \sup_{t\neq s\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{\|\omega(t)-\omega(s)\|_{\mathrm{E}}}{|t-s|^{\xi}(1+|t|+|s|)^{1/2}}<\infty}}\right\},

\displaystyle\Omega(\xi,\mathrm{E})

\displaystyle=\text{the closure of }\{\omega\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathrm{E}):\omega(0)=0\}\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{C}^{\xi}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R},\mathrm{E}).

The space
\Omega(\xi,\mathrm{E})
is a separable Banach space. Let us denote
\mathscr{F},
for the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
\Omega(\xi,\mathrm{E}).
For
\xi\in(0,1/2)
, there exists a Borel probability measure
\mathbb{P}
on
\Omega(\xi,\mathrm{E})
(see
[
4
]
). For
t\in\mathbb{R},
let
\mathscr{F}_{t}:=\sigma\{w_{s}:s\leq t\},
where
w_{t}
is the canonical process defined by the elements of
\Omega(\xi,\mathrm{E}).
Then there exists a family
\{\text{W}(t)\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
, which is
\mathrm{K}
-cylindrical Wiener process on a filtered probability space
(\Omega(\xi,\mathrm{E}),\mathscr{F},\{\mathscr{F}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}},\mathbb{P})
.

On the space
\Omega(\xi,\mathrm{E}),
we consider a flow
\theta=(\theta_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
defined by

\theta_{t}\omega(\cdot)=\omega(\cdot+t)-\omega(t),\ \omega\in\Omega(\xi,\mathrm{E}),\ t\in\mathbb{R}.

Lemma 3.1

(Proposition 6.10,
[
11
]
)
.

The process
\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(t),\ t\in\mathbb{R},
is a stationary Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process on
(\Omega(\xi,\mathrm{E}),\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})
. It is a solution of the equation

(3.1)

\displaystyle\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(t)+(\mu\mathrm{A}+\eta I)\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(t)\/\mathrm{d}\/t=\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathrm{W}(t),\ \ t\in\mathbb{R},

that is, for all
t\in\mathbb{R},

(3.2)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{t}e^{-(t-s)(\mu\mathrm{A}+\eta I)}\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathrm{W}(s),

\mathbb{P}
-a.s., where the integral is the Itô integral on the M-type 2 Banach space
\mathrm{X}
in the sense given in
[
5
]
.
In particular, for some constant
C
depending on
\mathrm{X}
, we have

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathrm{X}}}}\right]\leq C\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-2\eta s}\|e^{-\mu s\mathrm{A}}\|^{2}_{\gamma(\mathrm{K},\mathrm{X})}\/\mathrm{d}\/s.

Moreover,
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathrm{X}}}}\right]
tends to
0
as
\eta\to\infty.

Remark 3.2
.

By Proposition 4.1
[
38
]
, we deduce the following result for the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process given in Lemma
3.1
:

(3.4)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}\in\mathrm{L}^{q}(a,b;\mathrm{X})

where
q\in[1,\infty].

Since by Lemma
3.1
, the process
\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(t),\ t\in\mathbb{R}
is an
\mathrm{X}
-valued stationary and ergodic. Hence, by the Strong Law of Large Numbers (see
[
22
]
),

(3.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{-t}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathrm{X}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathrm{X}}}}\right],\ \ \mathbb{P}\text{-a.s., on }\ \mathrm{C}^{\xi}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R},\mathrm{X}).

Therefore by Lemma
3.1
, we find
\eta_{0}
such that for all
\eta\geq\eta_{0},
we have

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathrm{X}}}}\right]\leq\frac{\mu\alpha}{8},

where
\alpha
is the Darcy constant.

Let us denote

\Omega_{\eta}(\xi,\mathrm{E})=\{\omega\in\Omega(\xi,\mathrm{E}):\text{the equality \eqref{SLLN} holds true}\},

which is of full measure.
Therefore, we fix
\xi\in(\delta,1/2)
and set

\Omega:=\hat{\Omega}(\xi,\mathrm{E})=\bigcap^{\infty}_{n=0}\Omega_{n}(\xi,\mathrm{E}).

Consequently,
\Omega
also is of full measure.

Proposition 3.3
.

The quadruple
(\Omega,\hat{\mathscr{F}},\hat{\mathbb{P}},\hat{\theta})
is a metric DS, where
\hat{\mathscr{F}}
,
\hat{\mathbb{P}}
,
\hat{\theta}
are respectively the natural restrictions of
\mathscr{F}
,
\mathbb{P}
, and
\theta
to
\Omega.
For each
\omega\in\Omega,
the limit in (
3.5
) exists.

Now, we provide the important consequence of the estimates (
3.5
) and (
3.6
).

Corollary 3.4
.

For each
\omega\in\Omega,
there exists
t_{0}=t_{0}(\omega)\geq 0
, such that

(3.7)

\displaystyle\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{-t}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s\leq\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{-t}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathrm{X}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s\leq\frac{\alpha t}{2},\ \ t\geq t_{0}.

3.2.
Random dynamical system

Let us remember that Assumption
2.7
is satisfied and that
\delta
has the property stated there. We take fixed
\mu,\alpha,\beta>0
and some parameter
\eta\geq 0
. We also fix
\xi\in(\delta,1/2)
. Let us denote
\boldsymbol{v}^{\eta}(t)=\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(\omega)(t)
. For convenience, we write
\boldsymbol{v}^{\eta}(t)=\boldsymbol{v}(t)
and
\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(\omega)(t)=\boldsymbol{z}(t)
. Then
\boldsymbol{v}(t)
satisfies the following abstract system:

(3.8)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}&=-\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t)-\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t))-\alpha\boldsymbol{v}(t)-\beta\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t))+(\eta-\alpha)\boldsymbol{z}(t)+\boldsymbol{f},\\
\boldsymbol{v}(0)&=\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(0).\end{aligned}}}\right.

Since
\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(\omega)
is
\mathrm{X}
-valued process,
\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(\omega)(0)\in\mathbb{V}
. Note that for
\mathbb{P}
-a.a
\omega\in\Omega
, the system (
3.8
) is a deterministic one. In what follows, the definition of strong solution (in the deterministic sense) for the system (
3.8
) is provided.

Definition 3.5
.

For
r\in[1,3]
, assume that
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{H}
,
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
and

\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{\emph{loc}}([0,\infty);\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\emph{loc}}([0,\infty);\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})).

A function
\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)
is called a
weak solution
of the system (
3.8
) on time interval
[0,\infty)
, if

\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{C}([0,\infty);\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\emph{loc}}([0,\infty);\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{\mathrm{r}+1}_{\emph{loc}}([0,\infty);\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}),

\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\emph{loc}}([0,\infty);\mathbb{V}^{\prime})\text{ for }r\in[1,3)\ \text{ and }\ \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\emph{loc}}([0,\infty);\mathbb{V}^{\prime})+\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3}}_{\emph{loc}}([0,\infty);\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\frac{4}{3}})\text{ for }r=3,

and it satisfies

(i)

for any
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbb{V},

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\right\rangle=-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t)-\alpha\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t))+\beta\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(s)+\boldsymbol{z}(t))-(\eta-\alpha)\boldsymbol{z}(t)-\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\right\rangle,

for a.e.
t\in[0,\infty)
.

(ii)

\boldsymbol{v}(t)
satisfies the initial data:

\boldsymbol{v}(0)=\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z}(0).

Theorem 3.6
.

For
r\in[1,3]
, let
\eta\geq 0
,
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{H}
,
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
,

\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{\emph{loc}}([0,\infty);\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\emph{loc}}([0,\infty);\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})).

Then there exists a unique solution
\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)
to the system (
3.8
) in the sense of Definition
3.5
.

In addition, for
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{V}
and
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{H}
, there exists a unique strong solution
\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)
to the system (
2.32
) satisfying the following regularity:

\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{C}([0,\infty);\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\emph{loc}}([0,\infty);\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))\ \text{ and }\ \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\emph{loc}}([0,\infty);\mathbb{H}).

Proof.

Let us fix
T>0
. In order to complete the proof on interval
[0,\infty)
, it is enough to prove on interval
[0,T]
.

Step I.

Existence of weak solutions.
Since
\mathbb{V}
is a separable Hilbert space,
\mathcal{V}
is dense in
\mathbb{V}
and
\mathbb{V}
is dense in
\mathbb{H}
, there exists a set
\{w_{1},w_{2},\cdots,w_{n},\cdots\}\subset\mathcal{V}
which is complete orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{H}
. Let
\mathbb{H}_{n}
be the
n
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{H}
defined by
\mathbb{H}_{n}=\text{span}\{w_{1},w_{2},\cdots,w_{n}\}
with the norm inherited from
\mathbb{H}
. Let us denote the orthogonal projection from
\mathbb{H}
onto
\mathbb{H}_{n}
by
\mathrm{P}_{n}
, that is,
\boldsymbol{h}^{n}=\mathrm{P}_{n}\boldsymbol{h}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\boldsymbol{h},w_{j})w_{j}
, for
\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathbb{H}
. Let us consider the following approximate equation for the system (
3.8
) on finite dimensional space
\mathbb{H}_{n}
:

(3.10)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}&=\mathrm{P}_{n}\bigg[-\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}-\alpha\boldsymbol{v}^{n}-\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}+\boldsymbol{z})-\beta\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}+\boldsymbol{z})+(\eta-\alpha)\boldsymbol{z}+\boldsymbol{f}\bigg],\\
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(0)&=\mathrm{P}_{n}[\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z}(0)]:=\boldsymbol{v}_{0}^{n}.\end{aligned}}}\right.

We define
\mathrm{A}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}=\mathrm{P}_{n}\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}
,
\mathrm{B}_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}+\boldsymbol{z})=\mathrm{P}_{n}\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}+\boldsymbol{z})
and
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}+\boldsymbol{z})=\mathrm{P}_{n}\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}+\boldsymbol{z})
and consider the following system of ODEs:

(3.11)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}&=-\mu\mathrm{A}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)-\alpha\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)-\mathrm{B}_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t))-\beta\mathcal{C}_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t))\\
&\quad+(\eta-\alpha)\boldsymbol{z}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{f}^{n},\\
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(0)&=\boldsymbol{v}_{0}^{n},\end{aligned}}}\right.

Since
\mathrm{B}_{n}(\cdot)
and
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\cdot)
are locally Lipschitz, the system (
3.11
) has a unique local solution
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T^{*}];\mathbb{H}_{n})
, for some
0<T^{*}<T
. The following a priori estimates show that the time
T^{*}
can be extended to time
T
. Taking the inner product with
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(\cdot)
to the first equation of (
3.10
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle=-\mu\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}-\alpha\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}-b(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t))

\displaystyle\quad-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)}}\right)+(\eta-\alpha)(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t))+(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t))

\displaystyle=-\mu\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}-\alpha\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}-\beta\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}+b(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\boldsymbol{z}(t))

\displaystyle\quad+b(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\boldsymbol{z}(t))+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\boldsymbol{z}(t)}}\right)+((\eta-\alpha)\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t))

(3.12)

\displaystyle\quad+\langle\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\rangle.

Next, we estimate each term of RHS of (
3.2
) as

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b(\boldsymbol{v}^{n},\boldsymbol{v}^{n},\boldsymbol{z})}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|_{\mathbb{V}}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{\frac{3}{2}}_{\mathbb{V}}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}

(3.13)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu}{6}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+C\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{4}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}},

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v}^{n},\boldsymbol{z})}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{2}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|_{\mathbb{V}}\leq\frac{\mu}{6}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+C\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{4}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}},

(3.15)

\displaystyle\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}+\boldsymbol{z}),\boldsymbol{z}}}\right)}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\beta\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}+\boldsymbol{z}\|^{r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}+\boldsymbol{z}\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}+C\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}},

(3.16)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|((\eta-\alpha)\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t))+\langle\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\rangle}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu}{6}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+C\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}}+C\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}.

Combining (
3.2
)-(
3.16
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\mu\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}+\beta\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}+\beta\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}

(3.17)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}+C\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+C\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}+C\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}+C\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}}

An application of Gronwall’s inequality in (
3.2
) leads to

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}e^{\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\zeta)\|^{4}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}

(3.18)

\displaystyle\quad+C\int_{0}^{t}e^{\int_{\xi}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\zeta)\|^{4}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}\big[\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}}\big]\/\mathrm{d}\/\xi.

Furthermore, since
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))
,
\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(0)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}(0)\|_{\mathbb{H}}
and
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
, we have from (
3.2
) that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}<\infty,

from which we infer that

(3.19)

\displaystyle\{\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\text{ is a bounded sequence in }\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}).

Now, integrating (
3.2
) from
0
to
T
and using (
3.19
), we obtain

(3.20)

\displaystyle\{\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\text{ is a bounded sequence in }\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}).

Uniform estimate for
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}
:

When
r\in[1,3)
.
Let us first consider the case
r\in(1,3)
. For
r=1
, one can do in similar way, even it is much easier. For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and any arbitrary element
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
, using Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev’s embeddings, we have from (
3.10
)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)}}\right\rangle}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(\nabla\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}(t))}}\right|+\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\boldsymbol{\psi}(t))}}\right|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{\psi}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t))}}\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)}}\right\rangle}}\right|+(\eta-\alpha)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{\psi}(t))}}\right|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{\psi}(t))}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mu\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}+\beta\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r(1+\varepsilon)}}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\frac{1+\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}}}

\displaystyle\leq C\big[\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r(1+\varepsilon)}}\big]\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}},

which implies that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq C\big[\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r(1+\varepsilon)}}\big].

Now since
r<3
, we can choose
\varepsilon
small enough such that
r<1+\frac{2}{1+\varepsilon}
, so that

q:=\frac{2}{2-(r-1)(1+\varepsilon)}\in(1,\infty).

Let
q^{\prime}:=\frac{q}{q-1}=\frac{2}{(r-1)(1+\varepsilon)}
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
. Applying interpolation inequality and choosing
\lambda:=\frac{1}{r}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r(1+\varepsilon)}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{\lambda r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\lambda qr(1+\varepsilon)}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{(1-\lambda)r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{(1-\lambda)q^{\prime}r(1+\varepsilon)}}

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{q(1+\varepsilon)}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r-1}_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r-1}_{\mathbb{H}}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\big[\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2(r-1)}_{\mathbb{H}}\big]\/\mathrm{d}\/t,

which implies that
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{\prime})
.

When
r=3
.
For any arbitrary element
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{4}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4})
, using Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev embeddings, we have from (
3.10
)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)}}\right\rangle}}\right|\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq\mu\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(\nabla\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}(t))}}\right|\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{\psi}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t))}}\right|\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\boldsymbol{\psi}(t))}}\right|\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\beta\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)}}\right\rangle}}\right|\/\mathrm{d}\/t+(\eta-\alpha)\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{\psi}(t))}}\right|\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{\psi}(t))}}\right|\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\bigg[\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}^{\prime}}\bigg]\/\mathrm{d}\/t\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\beta\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t\bigg)^{\frac{3}{4}}\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|^{4}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t\bigg)^{\frac{1}{4}},

which gives that
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{\prime})+\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3}}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\frac{4}{3}})
.

Using (
3.19
), (
3.20
) and the
Banach-Alaoglu theorem
, we infer that there exists an element
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1})
and
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{\prime})
(for
r\in[1,3)
) and
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{\prime})+\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3}}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\frac{4}{3}})
(for
r=3
) such that

(3.21)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\xrightharpoonup{w^{*}}

\displaystyle\ \boldsymbol{v}\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}),

(3.22)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\xrightharpoonup{w}

\displaystyle\ \boldsymbol{v}\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}),

(3.23)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\xrightharpoonup{w}&\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{\prime}),&\text{ for }r\in[1,3),\\
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\xrightharpoonup{w}&\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{\prime})+\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3}}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\frac{4}{3}}),&\text{ for }r=3,\end{aligned}}}\right.

along a subsequence. The fact that
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
and
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{\prime})
imply
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{R}))
and
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{R}))
, where
\mathcal{O}_{R}=\mathcal{O}\cap\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|x|<R\}.
Since,
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{R}))
,
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{R}))
, the embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{R})\subset\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{R})\subset\mathbb{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{R})
is continuous and the embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{R})\subset\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{R})
is compact, then the
Aubin-Lions compactness lemma
implies that

(3.24)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\to\boldsymbol{v}\ \text{ strongly in }\ \mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{R})).

Next, we prove that
\boldsymbol{v}
is a solution to the system (
3.8
). Let
\psi:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuously differentiable function. Also, let
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbb{H}_{m}
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then from (
3.11
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle=-\mu\int_{0}^{T}(\mathrm{A}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t-\int_{0}^{T}(\mathrm{B}_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t

(3.25)

\displaystyle\quad-\beta\int_{0}^{T}(\mathcal{C}_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t+(\eta-\alpha)\int_{0}^{T}(\boldsymbol{z}^{n}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\int_{0}^{T}(\boldsymbol{f}^{n},\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t,

where we have used an integration by parts. Our next goal is to pass limit in (
3.2
) as
n\to\infty
. Due to the choice of
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbb{H}_{m}
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we can say that there exists
R\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\text{supp}\ \boldsymbol{\phi}\subset\mathcal{O}_{R}.
Since
\psi(\cdot)\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1})
, in view of (
3.23
), we obtain

(3.26)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/t-\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/t=\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}-\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/t\to 0,

as
n\to\infty
. Since
\psi(\cdot)\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{R}))
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{T}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t-\int_{0}^{T}(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}-\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{R}))}\|\psi(\cdot)\boldsymbol{\phi}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{R}))}

(3.27)

\displaystyle\to 0\text{ as }n\to\infty,

where we have used the strong convergence obtained in (
3.24
). Let us choose
n\geq m
so that
\mathbb{H}_{m}\subset\mathbb{H}_{n}
and
\mathrm{P}_{n}\boldsymbol{\phi}=\boldsymbol{\phi}
. Since
\psi(\cdot)\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
, it is immediate that

(3.28)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}(\mathrm{A}_{n}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t-\int_{0}^{T}(\!(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\!)\/\mathrm{d}\/t=\int_{0}^{T}(\!(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\!)\/\mathrm{d}\/t\to 0\text{ as }n\to\infty,

where we have used the weak convergence given in (
3.22
). To prove the convergence of third term of the right hand side of (
3.2
), we consider

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{T}(\mathrm{B}_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t-\int_{0}^{T}(\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{T}b(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t-\int_{0}^{T}b(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}\right|}_{:=B_{1}(n)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{T}b(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\int_{0}^{T}b(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)-v(t),\boldsymbol{z}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq B_{1}(n)+2\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}(\mathcal{O}_{R})}\|\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq B_{1}(n)+C\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{R})}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq B_{1}(n)+CT^{1/4}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4})}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}-\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{R}))}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}}}\right]

(3.29)

\displaystyle\to 0\text{ as }n\to\infty,

where we have used the convergence from Lemma
2.3
and (
3.24
). From Lemma
2.5
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}(\mathcal{C}_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/t

(3.30)

\displaystyle\to\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/t\text{ as }n\to\infty.

Furthermore, it is immediate that

(3.31)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}((\eta-\alpha)\boldsymbol{z}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{f}^{n},\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t=(\eta-\alpha)\int_{0}^{T}(\boldsymbol{z}^{n}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\int_{0}^{T}\langle\boldsymbol{f},\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi}\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/t.

Hence, on passing limit to (
3.2
) as
n\to\infty
with the help of (
3.26
)-(
3.31
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle=-\mu\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/t-\alpha\int_{0}^{T}(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t-\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/t

(3.32)

\displaystyle\quad-\beta\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/t+(\eta-\alpha)\int_{0}^{T}(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi})\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\boldsymbol{f},\psi(t)\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/t.

Since (
3.2
) holds for any
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\cup_{m=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{m}
and
\cup_{m=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{H}_{m}
is dense in
\mathbb{V}
, we have that (
3.2
) holds true for all
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbb{V}
and
\psi\in\mathrm{C}^{1}([0,T])
. Hence
\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)
solves (
3.9
) and satisfies first equation of (
3.8
).

Step II.

Energy equality:

When
r\in[1,3)
.
Since
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
and
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{\prime})
, we infer from Theorem 3, page 303,
[
24
]
that
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathbb{H})
, and hence the energy equality is satisfied.

When
r=3
.
Note that the embedding of
\mathbb{H}\subset\mathbb{V}^{\prime}
is continuous and
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
implies
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{\prime})
. Thus, we get
\boldsymbol{v},\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{\frac{r+1}{r}}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{\prime})
and then invoking Theorem 2, section 5.9.2
[
24
]
, it is immediate that
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathbb{V}^{\prime})
. Since
\mathbb{H}
is reflexive, using Proposition 1.7.1
[
17
]
, we obtain
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{C}_{w}([0,T];\mathbb{H})
and the map
t\mapsto\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}
is bounded, where
\mathrm{C}_{w}([0,T];\mathbb{H})
denotes the space of functions
\boldsymbol{v}:[0,T]\to\mathbb{H}
which are weakly continuous. Now, we show that
\boldsymbol{v}
satisfies energy equality and hence
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathbb{H})
. First define
\mathcal{V}_{T}:=\{\boldsymbol{\phi}:\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}_{0}(\mathcal{O}\times[0,T))\}
. Observe that, for each
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathcal{V}_{T}
,
\boldsymbol{\phi}(\cdot,T)=0
and
\mathcal{V}_{T}
is dense in
\mathrm{L}^{p}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathcal{O}))
(for case
p=2
see Lemma 2.6,
[
28
]
). For
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(0,T;\mathrm{X})
,
1\leq p<\infty
and
T>h>0
, the mollifier
\boldsymbol{v}_{h}
(in the sense of Friederichs) of
\boldsymbol{v}
is defined by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{h}(t)=\int_{0}^{T}j_{h}(t-\zeta)\boldsymbol{v}(\zeta)\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta,

where
j_{h}(\cdot)
is an infinite times differentiable function having support in
(-h,h)
, which is even and positive, such that
\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}j_{h}(\zeta)\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta=1
. In view of Lemma 2.5,
[
28
]
, we have that for
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(0,T;\mathrm{X})
with
1\leq p<\infty
,
\boldsymbol{v}_{h}\in\mathrm{C}^{k}([0,T];\mathrm{X})
for all
k\geq 0
and

(3.33)

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{h}-\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(0,T;\mathrm{X})}=0.

Furthermore, if
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathrm{L}^{p}(0,T;\mathrm{X})
converges to
\boldsymbol{v}
in the norm of
\mathrm{L}^{p}(0,T;\mathrm{X})
, then

(3.34)

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\|(\boldsymbol{v}_{m})_{h}-\boldsymbol{v}_{h}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(0,T;\mathrm{X})}=0.

We write the weak solution of (
3.8
) as

(3.35)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\biggl\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}+\mu\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}+\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z})+\beta\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z})-\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha\boldsymbol{v}+(\alpha-\eta)\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{\phi}}}\right)\biggr\}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta=0,

for all
t<T
and
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathcal{V}_{T}
. Let
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{V}_{T}
be a sequence converging to
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
, that is,
\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m}-\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\to 0
as
m\to\infty
. If
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
, then we also have the convergence
\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m}-\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4})}\to 0
as
m\to\infty
(by (
2.3
)). Choosing
\boldsymbol{\phi}=(\boldsymbol{v}_{m})_{h}=:\boldsymbol{v}_{m,h}
in (
3.35
), where
(\cdot)_{h}
is the mollification operator discussed above, for
0\leq t<T
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\biggl\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\boldsymbol{v}_{m,h}}}\right\rangle+\mu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}_{m,h}}}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z})+\beta\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z})-\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{v}_{m,h}}}\right\rangle

(3.36)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha\boldsymbol{v}+(\alpha-\eta)\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v}_{m,h}}}\right)\biggr\}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta=0.

Using (
3.34
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\boldsymbol{v}_{m,h}-\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}}\right|\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V}^{\prime})+\mathrm{L}^{\frac{4}{3}}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\frac{4}{3}})}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m,h}-\boldsymbol{v}_{h}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{4}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4})}\to 0,

and

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}_{m,h}-\nabla\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}}\right|\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m,h}-\boldsymbol{v}_{h}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\to 0,

as
m\to\infty
. Since
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}^{4}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4})
, using (
3.34
), we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z}),\boldsymbol{v}_{m,h}-\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}}\right|\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4})}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m,h}-\boldsymbol{v}_{h}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})}\to 0,

and
