\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z}),\boldsymbol{v}_{m,h}-\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}}\right|\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z}\|^{3}_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4})}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{m,h}-\boldsymbol{v}_{h}\|_{\mathrm{L}^{4}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4})}\to 0,

as
m\to\infty
. Similarly, using (
3.34
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\biggl\{-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{v}_{m,h}}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha\boldsymbol{v}+(\alpha-\eta)\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v}_{m,h}}}\right)\biggr\}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta\to\int_{0}^{t}\biggl\{-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha\boldsymbol{v}+(\alpha-\eta)\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}}\right)\biggr\}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta,

as
m\to\infty
. Hence, passing limit
m\to\infty
in (
3.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\biggl\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}}\right\rangle+\mu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z})+\beta\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z})-\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}}\right\rangle

(3.37)

\displaystyle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha\boldsymbol{v}+(\alpha-\eta)\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}}\right)\biggr\}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta=0.

Using (
3.33
) and similar arguments as above, we obtain the following convergence

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{0}^{t}\biggl\{\mu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}}\right)+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z})+\beta\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z})-\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha\boldsymbol{v}+(\alpha-\eta)\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}}\right)\biggr\}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta

(3.38)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\biggl\{\mu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}}\right)+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z})+\beta\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z})-\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{v}}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha\boldsymbol{v}+(\alpha-\eta)\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v}}}\right)\biggr\}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta.

Using integration by parts, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\boldsymbol{v},\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}_{h}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{v}(0),\boldsymbol{v}_{h}(0)}}\right)-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{h}(t)}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\zeta}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/j_{h}(\zeta-s)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{v}(\zeta),\boldsymbol{v}(s)}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/s\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{v}(0),\boldsymbol{v}_{h}(0)}}\right)-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{h}(t)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{v}(0),\boldsymbol{v}_{h}(0)}}\right)-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{h}(t)}}\right)

(3.39)

\displaystyle\to\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{v}(0),\boldsymbol{v}(0)}}\right)-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t)}}\right),

as
h\to 0
, where we have used the property of mollifiers and the fact that the kernel
j_{h}(s)
in the definition of mollifier is even in
(-h,h)
. From (
3.2
)-(
3.2
), we infer that
\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)
satisfies the energy equality:

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}+2\mu\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{v}(\zeta)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta+2\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{v}(\zeta)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}-2\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}(\zeta)+\boldsymbol{z}(\zeta)),\boldsymbol{v}(\zeta)\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta-2\beta\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(\zeta)+\boldsymbol{z}(\zeta)),\boldsymbol{v}(\zeta)\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/s

(3.40)

\displaystyle\quad+2\int_{0}^{t}\langle\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{v}(\zeta)\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta+2(\eta-\alpha)\int_{0}^{t}(\boldsymbol{z}(\zeta),\boldsymbol{v}(\zeta))\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta,

for all
t\in[0,T]
. Recalling that every weak solution of (
3.8
) is
\mathbb{H}
weakly continuous in time, all weak solutions satisfy the energy equality (
3.2
) and so, all weak solutions of (
3.8
) belong to
\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathbb{H})
(see
[
28
,
31
]
also).

Step III.

Uniqueness :
Define
\mathfrak{F}=\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}
, where
\boldsymbol{v}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{2}
are two weak solutions of the system (
3.8
). Then
\mathfrak{F}\in\mathrm{C}(0,T;\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{r+1}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1})
and satisfies

(3.41)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathfrak{F}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}&=-\mu\mathrm{A}\mathfrak{F}(t)-\alpha\mathfrak{F}(t)-\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t))+\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t))-\beta\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t))\\
&\quad+\beta\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\\
\mathfrak{F}(0)&=\textbf{0},\end{aligned}}}\right.

in the weak sense. From the above equation, using energy equality, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\mathfrak{F}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\mu\|\mathfrak{F}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\alpha\|\mathfrak{F}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t))-\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t)}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-b(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t),\mathfrak{F}(t))+b(\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t),\mathfrak{F}(t))

\displaystyle=b(\mathfrak{F}(t),\mathfrak{F}(t),\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t))+b(\mathfrak{F}(t),\mathfrak{F}(t),\boldsymbol{z}(t))\leq C\|\mathfrak{F}(t)\|^{1/2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathfrak{F}(t)\|^{3/2}_{\mathbb{V}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}}}\right]

(3.42)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\mathfrak{F}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+C\|\mathfrak{F}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t)\|^{4}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}}}\right].

Due to (
2.26
), fourth term on the left hand side of (
3.2
) is positive. Therefore,

(3.43)

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\mathfrak{F}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\leq C\|\mathfrak{F}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t)\|^{4}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}}}\right].

Applying Gronwall’s inequality and using the fact that
\boldsymbol{v}_{2},\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{L}^{4}(0,T;\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4})
and
\mathfrak{F}(0)=\textbf{0}
, we obtain that
\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t)=\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t)
, for all
t\in[0,T]
, which completes the proof of uniqueness.

Step IV.

Strong solution:
Let
r\in[1,3],

\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{V}
,
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{H}
and
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))
. Taking the inner product with
\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(\cdot)
to the first equation of (
3.10
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\mu\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}

\displaystyle=-b(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}^{n}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}^{n}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t))-\beta(\mathcal{C}\big(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\big),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t))

\displaystyle\quad+(\eta-\alpha)\big(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\big)+\big(\boldsymbol{f},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\big),

\displaystyle=-b(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t))-b(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\boldsymbol{z}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t))-b(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t))

(3.44)

\displaystyle\quad-b(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{z}(t),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t))-\beta(\mathcal{C}\big(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\big),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t))+\big((\eta-\alpha)\boldsymbol{z}(t)+\boldsymbol{f},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\big).

Using (
2.5
), Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we estimate

\displaystyle\big|b(\boldsymbol{v}^{n},\boldsymbol{v}^{n},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})+b(\boldsymbol{v}^{n},\boldsymbol{z},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})+b(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v}^{n},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})+b(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+C\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{4}_{\mathbb{V}}+C\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{4}_{\mathbb{V}}+C\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+C\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{3}_{\mathbb{V}},

and

\displaystyle|(\eta-\alpha)\big(\boldsymbol{z},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\big)+\big(\boldsymbol{f},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\big)|

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta-\alpha}}\right|\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\frac{\mu}{2}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+C\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+C\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}.

Next, using Hölder’s inequality, Young’s inequality and Sobolev’s embedding, we find

\displaystyle\big|(\mathcal{C}\big(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}+\boldsymbol{z}\big),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}+\boldsymbol{z}\|^{r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2r}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\frac{\mu}{14}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+C\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}+\boldsymbol{z}\|^{2r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2r}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu}{14}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{2r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2r}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2r}}}}\right]

(3.45)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu}{14}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{2r}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2r}}}}\right].

We estimate the final term from (
3.45
) using Gagliardo-Nirenberg’s inequality (Theorem 1,
[
50
]
) as

(3.46)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2r}}\leq\begin{cases}C\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{2r-2}_{\mathbb{V}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+C\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{4}_{\mathbb{V}},\ &\text{ for }r\in[1,3),\\
C\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|^{4}_{\mathbb{V}},\ \ \ \ &\text{ for }r=3.\end{cases}

We infer from the inequalities (
3.2
)-(
3.46
) that

(3.47)

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\leq\begin{cases}C[Q_{1}(t)\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\widetilde{Q}_{1}(t)],\ \text{ for }r\in[1,3),\\
C[Q_{2}(t)\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\widetilde{Q}_{2}(t)],\ \text{ for }r=3,\\
\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle Q_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}},\

\displaystyle Q_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=Q_{1}(t)+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{3}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2r}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{3}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{6}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}.

Note that
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{H}
,
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))
and
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
imply that

(3.48)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}Q_{1}(\zeta)\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta<\infty,\ \ \int_{0}^{T}\widetilde{Q}_{1}(\zeta)\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta<\infty,\ \ \int_{0}^{T}Q_{2}(\zeta)\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta<\infty\ \text{ and }\ \int_{0}^{T}\widetilde{Q}_{2}(\zeta)\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta<\infty.

Hence, an application of Gronwall’s inequality gives that
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))
.

For any arbitrary element
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathbb{H}
, using Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev embeddings, we have from (
3.8
)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\boldsymbol{\psi}}}\right)}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\boldsymbol{\psi})}}\right|+\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t),\boldsymbol{\psi})}}\right|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{\psi})}}\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\boldsymbol{\psi}}}\right)}}\right|+(\eta-\alpha)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{\psi})}}\right|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{\psi})}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2r}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}}}\right]\|\boldsymbol{\psi}\|_{\mathbb{H}},

which gives that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2r}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}}}\right]\/\mathrm{d}\/t.

It implies that
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
. From the fact that
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))
,
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
, and an application of the
Banach Alaoglu theorem
yield the existence of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))
and
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\xrightharpoonup{w^{*}}

\displaystyle\ \widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\xrightharpoonup{w}

\displaystyle\ \widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})),

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}^{n}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\xrightharpoonup{w}

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}).

In view of the uniqueness of weak limit, we have
\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}=\boldsymbol{v}
. Moreover,
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))
and
\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
implies that
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathbb{V})
, which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.7

(
[
38
]
)
.

Assume that, for
r\in[1,3]
and for some
T>0
fixed,
\boldsymbol{x}^{n}\to\boldsymbol{x}
in
\mathbb{H}
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{z}\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})),\ \ \boldsymbol{f}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{f}\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{V}^{\prime}.

Let us denote by
\boldsymbol{v}(t,\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{x}
, the solution of the problem (
3.8
) and by
\boldsymbol{v}(t,\boldsymbol{z}_{n})\boldsymbol{x}^{n}
, the solution of the problem (
3.8
) with
\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{x}
being replaced by
\boldsymbol{z}_{n},\boldsymbol{f}_{n},\boldsymbol{x}^{n}
, respectively. Then

\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{z}_{n})\boldsymbol{x}^{n}\to\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{x}\ \text{ in }\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V}).

In particular,
\boldsymbol{v}(T,\boldsymbol{z}_{n})\boldsymbol{x}^{n}\to\boldsymbol{v}(T,\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{x}
in
\mathbb{H}
.

Proof.

See the proof of Theorem 4.8,
[
38
]
.
∎

Lemma 3.8

(
[
39
]
)
.

Assume that, for
r\in[1,3]
and for some
T>0
fixed,
\boldsymbol{x}^{n}\to\boldsymbol{x}
in
\mathbb{V}
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{z}\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})),\ \ \boldsymbol{f}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{f}\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}).

Let us denote by
\boldsymbol{v}(t,\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{x}
, the solution of the problem (
3.8
) and by
\boldsymbol{v}(t,\boldsymbol{z}_{n})\boldsymbol{x}^{n}
, the solution of the problem (
3.8
) with
\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{x}
being replaced by
\boldsymbol{z}_{n},\boldsymbol{f}_{n},\boldsymbol{x}^{n}
, respectively. Then

\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{z}_{n})\boldsymbol{x}^{n}\to\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{x}\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{C}([0,T];\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})).

In particular,
\boldsymbol{v}(T,\boldsymbol{z}_{n})\boldsymbol{x}^{n}\to\boldsymbol{v}(T,\boldsymbol{z})\boldsymbol{x}
in
\mathbb{V}
.

Proof.

[

]
.

Definition 3.9
.

We define a map
\varphi^{\eta}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{V}
by

(3.49)

\displaystyle(t,\omega,\boldsymbol{x})\mapsto\boldsymbol{v}^{\eta}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(\omega)(t)\in\mathbb{V},

where
\boldsymbol{v}^{\eta}(t)=\boldsymbol{v}(t,\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(\omega)(t))(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(\omega)(0))
is a solution to the problem (
3.8
) with the initial condition
\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(\omega)(0).

Lemma 3.10

(
[
38
]
.)
.

If
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\geq 0
, then
\varphi^{\eta_{1}}=\varphi^{\eta_{2}}.

Using Lemma
3.10
, we denote
\varphi^{\eta}
by
\varphi
.

Lemma 3.11

(
[
39
]
)
.

(\varphi,\theta)
is a random dynamical system.

Let us now define, for
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{V},\ \omega\in\Omega,
and
t\geq s,

(3.50)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}(t,s;\omega,\boldsymbol{x}):=\varphi(t-s;\theta_{s}\omega)\boldsymbol{x}=\boldsymbol{v}\big(t,s;\omega,\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z}(s)\big)+\boldsymbol{z}(t),

then for each
s\in\mathbb{R}
and each
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{V},
the process
\boldsymbol{u}(t),\ t\geq s,
is a solution to the problem (
2.32
).

4.
Random attractors for 2D SCBF equations in
\mathbb{V}

The existence of random attractors in
\mathbb{V}
for the 2D SCBF equations (
2.32
) is established in this section. We consider the RDS
\varphi
over the metric DS
(\Omega,\hat{\mathscr{F}},\hat{\mathbb{P}},\hat{\theta})
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{v}(t,\boldsymbol{v}_{0})
be the unique solution to the initial value problem (
3.8
) with initial condition
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}
, and with a deterministic function
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{+};\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))
. For
r\in[1,3]
and for
T>0,
if
\boldsymbol{y}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{y}
in
\mathbb{V}
weakly, then

(4.1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}(t,\boldsymbol{y}_{n})\xrightharpoonup{w}\boldsymbol{v}(t,\boldsymbol{y})\ \text{ in }\

\displaystyle\mathbb{V},

(4.2)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{y}_{n})\xrightharpoonup{w}\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{y})\ \text{ in }\

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})).

Proof.

Assume that
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an
\mathbb{V}
-valued sequence such that
\boldsymbol{y}_{n}
converges to
\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{V}
weakly. Let
\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)=\boldsymbol{v}(t,\boldsymbol{y}_{n})
and
\boldsymbol{v}(t)=\boldsymbol{v}(t,\boldsymbol{y})
. Let us first prove (
4.2
). Since
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{n}\}_{n}
is a bounded sequence in
\mathbb{V}
, we infer that

(4.3)

\displaystyle\text{ the sequence }\ \{\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\ \text{ is bounded in }\ \mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})).

Hence, by the Banach-Alaoglu theorem, there exists a subsequence
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{n^{\prime}}\}_{n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
, such that, as
n^{\prime}\to\infty,

(4.4)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{n^{\prime}}\xrightharpoonup{w^{*}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}

\displaystyle\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V}),

(4.5)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{n^{\prime}}\xrightharpoonup{w}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}

\displaystyle\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})),

as
n^{\prime}\to\infty
. For
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})
, we consider

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\bigg(\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\bigg)\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq\mu\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle+\beta\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{2r}}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta-\alpha}}\right|\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}\right)^{1/2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{4}\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}\right)^{1/2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}\right)^{1/2}+T^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2r}\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg\}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and thus, we obtain
\big\|\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}_{n}}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\big\|_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H})}\leq C,
for some
C>0
independent of
n
. Hence by the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, for all
0\leq t\leq t+a\leq T
,
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})
and
n\in\mathbb{N},
we have

(4.6)

\displaystyle|(\!(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t+a)-\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t),\boldsymbol{\phi})\!)|\leq\int_{t}^{t+a}\bigg|\bigg(\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(s)}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{\phi}\bigg)\bigg|\/\mathrm{d}\/s\leq C\|\boldsymbol{\phi}\|_{\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})}\sqrt{a}.

For
\boldsymbol{\phi}=\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t+a)-\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)
and for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
, from (
4.6
) we get

\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t+a)-\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\leq C\sqrt{a}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t+a)-\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})}.

Integrating from
0
to
T-a
and, using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and (
4.3
), we further deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-a}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t+a)-\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{a}\int_{0}^{T-a}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t+a)-\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{a}(T-a)^{1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int_{0}^{T-a}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t+a)-\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A})}\/\mathrm{d}\/t}}\right)^{1/2}

(4.7)

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{a}.

Furthermore, we have

(4.8)

\displaystyle\lim_{a\to 0}\sup_{n}\int_{0}^{T-a}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t+a)-\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{R})}\/\mathrm{d}\/t=0,

for all
R>0
, where
\mathcal{O}_{R}=\mathcal{O}\cap\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|x|\leq R\}.

Due to compact embedding
\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{R})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}^{1}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{R})
, we have (see Theorem 13.3,
[
56
]
)

\boldsymbol{v}_{n^{\prime}}\to\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\ \text{ strongly in }\ \mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{R})).

The convergences given above can be used to pass limit in the equation (
3.8
) for
\boldsymbol{v}_{n}
and deduce that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
is a solution of (
3.8
) with initial data
\boldsymbol{y}
at time
0
. Since the system (
3.8
) has a unique solution, we infer that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}=\boldsymbol{v}.
By using a standard contradiction argument, we conclude that the whole sequence

\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\to\boldsymbol{v}\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))\text{ weakly},

and hence the convergence given in (
4.2
) is established.

Let us now prove the convergence (
4.1
). Take any function
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathcal{V}.
Then, by (
4.4
), for a.e.
t\in[0,T],
we have

(\!(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t),\boldsymbol{\phi})\!)\to(\!(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{\phi})\!).

Since
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a bounded sequence in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V}),\{(\!(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(\cdot),\boldsymbol{\phi})\!)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly bounded on
[0,T].
Also, the estimate (
4.6
) shows that the sequence
\{(\!(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(\cdot),\boldsymbol{\phi})\!)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly equicontinuous on
[0,T].
Hence, there exists a subsequence
\{(\!(\boldsymbol{v}_{n^{\prime}}(\cdot),\boldsymbol{\phi})\!)\}_{n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\{(\!(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(\cdot),\boldsymbol{\phi})\!)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
(by the Arzela-Ascoli theorem), such that

(\!(\boldsymbol{v}_{n^{\prime}}(\cdot),\boldsymbol{\phi})\!)\to(\!(\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot),\boldsymbol{\phi})\!)\text{ uniformly on }[0,T].

Again, using the standard contradiction argument, we assert that

(\!(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(\cdot),\boldsymbol{\phi})\!)\to(\!(\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot),\boldsymbol{\phi})\!)\text{ uniformly on }[0,T].

Using the density of
\mathcal{V}
in
\mathbb{V}
and
\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}<\infty
, for any
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbb{V},

\displaystyle(\!(\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(\cdot),\boldsymbol{\phi})\!)\to(\!(\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot),\boldsymbol{\phi})\!)\text{ uniformly on }[0,T],

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.2

(Lemma 4.1
[
39
]
)
.

For each
\omega\in\Omega,

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to-\infty}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\ e^{\alpha t+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{t}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}=0.

Lemma 4.3

(Lemma 4.2
[
39
]
)
.

For each
\omega\in\Omega,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\bigg\{1+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(s)\|^{r+1}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\mathbb{V}}\bigg\}e^{\alpha t+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{t}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}\/\mathrm{d}\/t<\infty.

Definition 4.4
.

A function
\kappa:\Omega\to(0,\infty)
belongs to class
\mathfrak{K}
if and only if

(4.9)

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to\infty}[\kappa(\theta_{-t}\omega)]^{2}e^{-\alpha t+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{-t}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s}=0,

where
\alpha
is Darcy’s constant.

We denote by
\hat{\mathfrak{DK}},
the class of all closed and bounded random sets
\mathrm{D}
on
\mathbb{V}
such that the radius function
\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto\kappa(\mathrm{D}(\omega)):=\sup\{\|x\|_{\mathbb{V}}:x\in\mathrm{D}(\omega)\}
belongs to the class
\mathfrak{K}.

By Corollary
3.4
, we infer that the constant functions belong to
\mathfrak{K}
.
The class
\mathfrak{K}
is closed with respect to sum, multiplication by a constant and if
\kappa\in\mathfrak{K},0\leq\bar{\kappa}\leq\kappa,
then
\bar{\kappa}\in\mathfrak{K}.

Lemma 4.5

(Proposition 4.4
[
39
]
)
.

Define functions
\kappa_{i}:\Omega\to(0,\infty),\ i=1,2,3,4,5,6,
by the following formulae, for
\omega\in\Omega,

\displaystyle[\kappa_{1}(\omega)]^{2}

\displaystyle:=\|\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(0)\|_{\mathbb{V}},\ \ [\kappa_{2}(\omega)]^{2}:=\sup_{s\leq 0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\ e^{\alpha s+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{s}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta},

\displaystyle[\kappa_{3}(\omega)]^{2}

\displaystyle:=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\ e^{\alpha t+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{t}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}\/\mathrm{d}\/t,

\displaystyle[\kappa_{4}(\omega)]^{2}

\displaystyle:=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(t)\|^{r+1}_{\mathbb{V}}\ e^{\alpha t+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{t}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}\/\mathrm{d}\/t,

\displaystyle[\kappa_{5}(\omega)]^{2}

\displaystyle:=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(t)\|^{4}_{\mathbb{V}}\ e^{\alpha t+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{t}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}\/\mathrm{d}\/t,\ \ [\kappa_{6}(\omega)]^{2}:=\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{\alpha t+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{t}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}\/\mathrm{d}\/t.

Then all these functions belongs to class
\mathfrak{K}.

Theorem 4.6
.

Assume that, for
r\in[1,3],

\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{V},\ \boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{H}
and Assumption
2.7
holds. Then there exists a closed and bounded
\hat{\mathfrak{DK}}
-random absorbing sets in
\mathbb{V}
corresponding to the RDS
\varphi.

Proof.

Let
{\mathrm{D}}(\omega)\in\hat{\mathfrak{DK}}
. Let
\kappa_{\mathrm{D}}(\omega)
be the radius of
{\mathrm{D}}(\omega)
, that is,
\kappa_{\mathrm{D}}(\omega):=\sup\{\|x\|_{\mathbb{V}}:x\in{\mathrm{D}}(\omega)\},\ \omega\in\Omega.
Let
\omega\in\Omega
be fixed. For given
s\leq 0
and
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{V}
, let
\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)
be the unique solution of (
3.8
) on time interval
[s,\infty)
with the initial condition
\boldsymbol{v}(s)=\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z}(s).

Multiplying the first equation of (
3.8
) by
\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)
and integrating the resulting equation over
\mathcal{O}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}=

\displaystyle-\mu\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}-\alpha\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}-b(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t))

\displaystyle-\beta(\mathcal{C}\big(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\big),\boldsymbol{v}(t))+(\eta-\alpha)\big(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)+\big(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mu\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}-\alpha\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}-\beta\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}-b(\boldsymbol{v}(t),\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t))

\displaystyle-b(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t))+\beta(\mathcal{C}\big(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\big),\boldsymbol{z}(t))+(\eta-\alpha)\big(\boldsymbol{z}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)

(4.10)

\displaystyle+\big(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big).

Using Hölder’s inequality, Young’s inequality, Sobolev’s embedding and (
2.3
), we have

\displaystyle|b(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v})|

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{2}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{V}}\leq\sqrt{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{V}}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{V}}\leq\frac{\mu}{4}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\frac{2}{\mu}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}},

\displaystyle|b(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v})|

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{2}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{4}}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{V}}\leq\sqrt{2}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{V}}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{V}}\leq\frac{\mu}{4}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\frac{2}{\mu}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}},

\displaystyle|\beta\big\langle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z}),\boldsymbol{z}\big\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\beta\|\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z}\|^{r}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z}\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}+\frac{\beta(2r)^{r}}{(r+1)^{r+1}}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{z}\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}+C\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{r+1}_{\mathbb{V}},

\displaystyle|(\eta-\alpha)\big(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{v}\big)+\big(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{v}\big)|

\displaystyle\leq\big[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\eta-\alpha}}\right|\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\big]\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{(\eta-\alpha)^{2}}{\alpha}\|\boldsymbol{z}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}.

Thus from (
4
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\mu\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\beta\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}

(4.11)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\mu}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\ \|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+C\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\mathbb{V}}+C\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r+1}_{\mathbb{V}}+C\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+C\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}.

We infer from the classical Gronwall inequality that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}e^{\alpha s+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{s}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}+C\int_{s}^{0}e^{\alpha t+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{t}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}\biggl\{\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r+1}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\biggr\}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\boldsymbol{x}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}e^{\alpha s+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{s}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}+2\|\boldsymbol{z}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}e^{\alpha s+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{s}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}+C\int_{s}^{0}e^{\alpha t+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{t}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}

(4.12)

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg\{\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r+1}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\biggr\}\/\mathrm{d}\/t.

For
\omega\in\Omega,
let us set

\displaystyle[\kappa_{11}(\omega)]^{2}

\displaystyle=2+2\sup_{s\leq 0}\bigg\{\|\boldsymbol{z}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\ e^{\alpha s+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{s}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}\bigg\}+C\int_{-\infty}^{0}e^{\alpha t+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{t}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}\bigg\{\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\mathbb{V}}

(4.13)

\displaystyle\quad+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{r+1}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\bigg\}\/\mathrm{d}\/t,

(4.14)

\displaystyle\kappa_{12}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(0)\|_{\mathbb{V}}.

By Lemmas
4.3
and
4.5
, it is immediate that both
\kappa_{11}
and
\kappa_{12}
belong to the class
\mathfrak{K}
.
Let
\omega\in\Omega
be fixed. Since
\kappa_{\mathrm{D}}(\omega)\in\mathfrak{K}
, there exists
t_{\mathrm{D}}(\omega)\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle[\kappa_{\mathrm{D}}(\theta_{-t}\omega)]^{2}e^{-\alpha t+\frac{4}{\mu}\int_{-t}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/s}\leq 1\ \text{ for }\ t\geq t_{\mathrm{D}}(\omega).

Thus, if
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathrm{D}}(\theta_{-t}\omega)
and
s\leq-t_{\mathrm{D}}(\omega),
then, by (
4
), we get

(4.15)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}(0,s;\omega,\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z}(s))\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\kappa_{11}(\omega).

Using a similar argument, we observe that for all
t\in[-1,0]
and for any
\omega\in\Omega
, there exists a
\kappa_{13}(\omega)\geq 0
such that

(4.16)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}(t,s;\omega,\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(s))\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\kappa_{13}(\omega),

for all
s\leq-(t_{\mathrm{D}}(\omega)+1).

Furthermore, integrating (
4
) over
(-1,0)
, we find that for any
\omega\in\Omega
, there exists a
\kappa_{14}(\omega)\geq 0
such that

(4.17)

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\int_{-1}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t\leq\kappa_{14}(\omega),

for all
s\leq-(t_{\mathrm{D}}(\omega)+1).
Taking the inner product with
\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(\cdot)
to the first equation of (
3.8
), similar calculations as we have performed for (
3.47
), provide us

(4.18)

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\begin{cases}C[Q_{1}(t)\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\widetilde{Q}_{1}(t)],\ \text{ for }r\in[1,3),\\
C[Q_{2}(t)\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\widetilde{Q}_{2}(t)],\ \text{ for }r=3,\\
\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle Q_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}},\

\displaystyle Q_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=Q_{1}(t)+\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{3}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2r}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{4}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{3}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{6}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}.

Applying Gronwall’s inequality on the time interval
[-1,0]
in (
4.18
), we get for
i\in\{1,2\}

(4.19)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}e^{\int_{t}^{0}Q_{i}(s)\/\mathrm{d}\/s}+\int_{t}^{0}\widetilde{Q}_{i}(s)e^{\int_{t}^{0}Q_{i}(\tau)\/\mathrm{d}\/\tau}\/\mathrm{d}\/s,\quad t\in[-1,0].

Integrating (
4.19
) from
-1
to
0
, we obtain for
i\in\{1,2\}

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-1}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}e^{\int_{t}^{0}Q_{i}(s)\/\mathrm{d}\/s}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\int_{-1}^{0}\int_{t}^{0}\widetilde{Q}_{i}(s)e^{\int_{t}^{0}Q_{i}(\tau)\/\mathrm{d}\/\tau}\/\mathrm{d}\/s\/\mathrm{d}\/t

(4.20)

\displaystyle\leq e^{\int_{-1}^{0}Q_{i}(s)\/\mathrm{d}\/s}\int_{-1}^{0}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+e^{\int_{-1}^{0}Q_{i}(s)\/\mathrm{d}\/s}\int_{-1}^{0}\widetilde{Q}_{i}(t)\/\mathrm{d}\/t.

In view of (
3.4
), (
4.16
) and (
4.17
), we infer from (
4
) that for any
r\in[1,3]
and for any
\omega\in\Omega,
there exists
\kappa_{15}(\omega)\geq 0
such that

(4.21)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}(0,\omega;s,\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z}(s))\|_{\mathbb{V}}\leq\kappa_{15}(\omega),

for any
s\leq-(t_{\mathrm{D}}(\omega)+1)
. Moreover, we have

(4.22)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{u}(0,s;\omega,\boldsymbol{x})\|_{\mathbb{V}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}(0,s;\omega,\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{z}(s))\|_{\mathbb{V}}+\|\boldsymbol{z}(\omega)(0)\|_{\mathbb{V}}\leq\kappa_{15}(\omega)+\kappa_{12}(\omega):=\kappa_{16}(\omega).

This implies that the ball
B_{\mathbb{V}}(0,\kappa_{16}(\omega))\in\hat{\mathfrak{DK}}
, that is, the ball in
\mathbb{V}
centered at the origin and of radius
\kappa_{16}
, absorbs
{\mathrm{D}}(\omega)
.
∎

Theorem 4.7
.

Suppose that
r\in[1,3]
, the domain
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfies Assumption
1.1
, Assumption
2.7
is satisfied and
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{H}
, then the RDS
\varphi
generated by (
2.32
) on
\mathbb{V}
is
\mathbb{V}
-asymptotically compact.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathrm{D}(\omega)\in\hat{\mathfrak{DK}}
and
\textbf{B}(\omega)\in\hat{\mathfrak{DK}}
is such that
\textbf{B}(\omega)
absorbs
\mathrm{D}(\omega)
. Let us fix
\omega\in\Omega
and take a sequence of positive numbers
\{t_{n}\}^{\infty}_{n=1}
such that
t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq t_{3}\leq\cdots
and
t_{n}\to\infty
. We take a
\mathbb{V}
-valued sequence
\{\boldsymbol{x}_{n}\}^{\infty}_{n=1}
such that
\boldsymbol{x}_{n}\in\mathrm{D}(\theta_{-t}\omega),
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}.

Step I.

Reduction.
Since
\textbf{B}(\omega)
absorbs
\mathrm{D}(\omega)
, we obtain

(4.23)

\displaystyle\varphi(t_{n},\theta_{-t_{n}}\omega,\mathrm{D}(\theta_{-t_{n}}\omega))\subset\textbf{B}(\omega),

for sufficient large
n\in\mathbb{N}.
Since
\textbf{B}(\omega)\subset\mathbb{V}
, is a bounded set, which implies that
\textbf{B}(\omega)
is weakly pre-compact in
\mathbb{V}
, without loss of generality, we may assume that (
4.23
) holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and, for some
\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\in\mathbb{V},

(4.24)

\displaystyle\varphi(t_{n},\theta_{-t_{n}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n})\to\boldsymbol{y}_{0}\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{V}\ \text{ weakly}.

Since
\boldsymbol{z}(0)\in\mathbb{V},
in order to complete the proof, we only need to show that for some subsequence
\{n^{\prime}\}\subset\mathbb{N}
(for more details, see Theorem 5.9 of
[
38
]
)

(4.25)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}_{0}-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|_{\mathbb{V}}\geq\limsup_{n^{\prime}\to\infty}\|\varphi(t_{n^{\prime}},\theta_{-t_{n^{\prime}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{\prime}})-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|_{\mathbb{V}},

as the norm is weakly lower semicontinuous.

Step II.

Construction of a negative trajectory.
That is, the construction of a sequence
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{n}\}^{0}_{n=-\infty}
such that
\boldsymbol{y}_{n}\in\boldsymbol{B}(\theta_{n}\omega),n\in\mathbb{Z}^{-},
and

\boldsymbol{y}_{k}=\varphi(k-n,\theta_{n}\omega,\boldsymbol{y}_{n}),\ \ \ n<k\leq 0.

Since
\boldsymbol{B}(\omega)
is bounded and closed in
\mathbb{V}
, it is also weakly compact in
\mathbb{V}
. Therefore, analogous to the proof of Proposition 5.9 in
[
38
]
, for each
k=1,2,\cdots,
we can construct a subsequence
\{n^{(k)}\}\subset\{n^{(k-1)}\}
and
\boldsymbol{y}_{-k}\in\textbf{B}(\theta_{-k}\omega),
such that
\varphi(1,\theta_{-k}\omega,\boldsymbol{y}_{-k})=\boldsymbol{y}_{-k+1}
and

(4.26)

\displaystyle\varphi(-k+t_{n^{(k)}},\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})\xrightharpoonup{w}\boldsymbol{y}_{-k}\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{V}\ \text{ as }\ n^{(k)}\to\infty.

Now, using the cocycle property of RDS
\varphi
, with
t=k,\ s=-k+t_{n^{(k)}}
and
\omega
being replaced by
\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,
we get

(4.27)

\displaystyle\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}},\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega)=\varphi(k,\theta_{-k}\omega)\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}}-k,\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega),\ \ \ k\in\mathbb{N}.

Hence, using the convergence (
4.1
) of Lemma
4.1
with (
4.26
), we obtain

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}_{-j}=

\displaystyle\ \textrm{w}_{\mathbb{V}}\text{-}\lim_{n^{(k)}\to\infty}\varphi(-j+t_{n^{(k)}},\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \textrm{w}_{\mathbb{V}}\text{-}\lim_{n^{(k)}\to\infty}\varphi\big(-j+k,\theta_{-k}\omega,\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}}-k,\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \varphi\bigg(-j+k,\theta_{-k}\omega,\big(\textrm{w}_{\mathbb{V}}\text{-}\lim_{n^{(k)}\to\infty}\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}}-k,\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})\big)\bigg)

(4.28)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \varphi(-j+k,\theta_{-k}\omega,\boldsymbol{y}_{-k}),

where
\textrm{w}_{\mathbb{V}}\text{-}\lim
represents the limit in the weak topology on
\mathbb{V}
given in (
4.1
) of Lemma
4.1
.
The same proof provides a more general property:

\varphi(j,\theta_{-k}\omega,\boldsymbol{y}_{-k})=\boldsymbol{y}_{-k+j},\ \text{ if }\ 0\leq j\leq k.

More precisely, in (
4
),
\boldsymbol{y}_{-j}=\boldsymbol{u}(-j,-k;\omega,\boldsymbol{y}_{-k}),
where
\boldsymbol{u}
is defined by (
3.50
).

Step III.

Proof of (
4.25
).
From (
3.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\/\mathrm{d}\/}{\/\mathrm{d}\/t}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\bigg\{\big((\eta-\alpha)\boldsymbol{z}(t)+\boldsymbol{f},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)-\big(\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-\beta\big(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(t)+\boldsymbol{z}(t)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big)-\mu\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}\bigg\}.

Then, using the variation of constant formula, we have for
t_{0}\leq\tau\leq t
,

(4.29)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}=\|\boldsymbol{v}(\tau)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}e^{-\alpha(t-\tau)}+2\int_{\tau}^{t}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\bigg\{((\eta-\alpha)\boldsymbol{z}(s)+\boldsymbol{f},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(s))

\displaystyle-(\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}(s)+\boldsymbol{z}(s)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(s))-\beta(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(s)+\boldsymbol{z}(s)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(s))-\mu\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}\bigg\}\/\mathrm{d}\/s.

From now onward (until explicitly stated), we fix
k\in\mathbb{N},
and consider the problem (
2.32
) on the interval
[-k,0].
From (
3.50
) and (
4.27
), with
t=0
and
s=-k,
we have

\displaystyle\|\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}},\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}=

\displaystyle\|\varphi\big(k,\theta_{-k}\omega,\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}}-k,\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})\big)-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}

(4.30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}\big(0,-k;\omega,\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}}-k,\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})-\boldsymbol{z}(-k)\big)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}.

Let
\boldsymbol{v}
be the solution to (
3.8
) on
[-k,\infty)
with
\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{z}_{\eta}(\cdot,\omega)
and the initial condition at time
-k:

\boldsymbol{v}(-k)=\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}}-k,\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})-\boldsymbol{z}(-k).

Also, we can write

\boldsymbol{v}(s)=\boldsymbol{v}\big(s,-k;\omega,\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}}-k,\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})-\boldsymbol{z}(-k)\big),\ s\geq-k.

Using (
4.29
) with
t=0
and
\tau=-k
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}},\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}

\displaystyle=e^{-\alpha k}\|\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}}-k,\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})-\boldsymbol{z}(-k)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+2\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}\bigg\{\big((\eta-\alpha)\boldsymbol{z}(s)+\boldsymbol{f},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(s)\big)

\displaystyle\quad-\big(\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{v}(s)+\boldsymbol{z}(s)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(s)\big)-\beta\big(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(s)+\boldsymbol{z}(s)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(s)\big)-\mu\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}\bigg\}\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle=e^{-\alpha k}\|\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}}-k,\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})-\boldsymbol{z}(-k)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}+2\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}\bigg\{\big((\eta-\alpha)\boldsymbol{z}(s)+\boldsymbol{f},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(s)\big)

\displaystyle\quad-b\big(\boldsymbol{v}(s),\boldsymbol{v}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(s)\big)-b\big(\boldsymbol{v}(s),\boldsymbol{z}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(s)\big)-b\big(\boldsymbol{z}(s),\boldsymbol{v}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(s)\big)

(4.31)

\displaystyle\quad-b\big(\boldsymbol{z}(s),\boldsymbol{z}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(s)\big)-\beta\big(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}(s)+\boldsymbol{z}(s)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(s)\big)-\mu\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}(s)\|_{\mathbb{V}}^{2}\bigg\}\/\mathrm{d}\/s.

Analogous to the methods used in the proof of Theorem 5.9,
[
38
]
, using (
4.18
), we can deduce that there exists a non negative function
h\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(-\infty,0)
such that

(4.32)

\displaystyle\limsup_{n^{(k)}\to\infty}\ e^{-\alpha k}\|\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}}-k,\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})-\boldsymbol{z}(-k)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\leq\int_{-\infty}^{-k}h(s)\ \/\mathrm{d}\/s,\ k\in\mathbb{N}.

Let us denote

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{v}\big(s,-k;\omega,\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}}-k,\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega)\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}}-\boldsymbol{z}(-k)\big),\ s\in(-k,0),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{v}\big(s,-k;\omega,\boldsymbol{y}_{-k}-\boldsymbol{z}(-k)\big),\ s\in(-k,0).

By Lemma
4.1
and the convergence (
4.26
), we conclude that

(4.33)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(\cdot)\text{ converges to }\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(\cdot)\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{2}(-k,0;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))\text{ weakly}.

Note that
\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathbb{H}
implies
s\mapsto e^{\alpha s}\boldsymbol{f}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(-k,0;\mathbb{H}).
Thus, using (
4.33
), we obtain

(4.34)

\displaystyle\lim_{n^{(k)}\to\infty}\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}\big(\boldsymbol{f},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s)\big)\ \/\mathrm{d}\/s=\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}\big(\boldsymbol{f},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)\ \/\mathrm{d}\/s.

Now,
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})
(see (
3.4
)) implies that
s\mapsto e^{\alpha s}\boldsymbol{z}(s)\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(-k,0;\mathbb{H}).
Therefore, using (
4.33
), we obtain

(4.35)

\displaystyle\lim_{n^{(k)}\to\infty}\int_{-k}^{0}(\eta-\alpha)e^{\alpha s}\big(\boldsymbol{z}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/s=\int_{-k}^{0}(\eta-\alpha)e^{\alpha s}\big(\boldsymbol{z}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/s.

Using
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))
(see (
3.4
)), and (
2.6
), we obtain that
s\mapsto e^{\alpha s}\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{z}(s))\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(-k,0;\mathbb{H}).
Hence, using (
4.33
), we obtain

(4.36)

\displaystyle\lim_{n^{(k)}\to\infty}\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}b\big(\boldsymbol{z}(s),\boldsymbol{z}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/s=\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}b\big(\boldsymbol{z}(s),\boldsymbol{z}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/s.

Since we have the convergence (
4.26
), by the Banach-Alaoglu theorem, we can find a subsequence of
\{\varphi(-k+t_{n^{(k)}},\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})\}
(denoted as the same) such that

(4.37)

\displaystyle\varphi(-k+t_{n^{(k)}},\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})\ \text{ converges to }\ \boldsymbol{y}_{-k}\ \text{ in }\ \mathbb{H}\ \text{ strongly as }n^{(k)}\to\infty.

By Theorem
3.7
, we have

(4.38)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(\cdot)\text{ converges to }\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(\cdot)\ \text{ in }\ \mathrm{L}^{2}(-k,0;\mathbb{V})\text{ strongly}.

We have the convergences (
4.33
) and (
4.38
) with
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{V})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,T;\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{A}))
(see (
3.4
)). Therefore, by Theorems
2.4
and
2.6
, we conclude that

(4.39)

\displaystyle\lim_{n^{(k)}\to\infty}\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}b\big(\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s),\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle=\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}b\big(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s),\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/s,

(4.40)

\displaystyle\lim_{n^{(k)}\to\infty}\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}b\big(\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s),\boldsymbol{z}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle=\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}b\big(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s),\boldsymbol{z}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/s,

(4.41)

\displaystyle\lim_{n^{(k)}\to\infty}\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}b\big(\boldsymbol{z}(s),\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle=\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}b\big(\boldsymbol{z}(s),\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/s,

(4.42)

\displaystyle\lim_{n^{(k)}\to\infty}\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}\big(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s)+\boldsymbol{z}(s)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/s

\displaystyle=\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}\big(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)+\boldsymbol{z}(s)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)\/\mathrm{d}\/s.

From (
4.33
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mu\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}}}\right]\/\mathrm{d}\/s\leq\liminf_{n^{(k)}\to\infty}\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mu\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}}}\right]\/\mathrm{d}\/s.

We can also write the above inequality as

\displaystyle\limsup_{n^{(k)}\to\infty}\bigg\{-\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mu\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n^{(k)}}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}}}\right]\/\mathrm{d}\/s\bigg\}

(4.43)

\displaystyle\leq-\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mu\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}}}\right]\/\mathrm{d}\/s.

From (
4
), (
4.32
), (
4.34
)-(
4.36
), (
4.39
)-(
4.42
), and inequality (
4
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n^{(k)}\to\infty}\|\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}},\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\infty}^{-k}h(s)\/\mathrm{d}\/s+2\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}\bigg\{\big((\eta-\alpha)\boldsymbol{z}(s)+\boldsymbol{f},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)-b\big(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s),\boldsymbol{v}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)

\displaystyle\quad-b\big(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s),\boldsymbol{z}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)-b\big(\boldsymbol{z}(s),\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)-b\big(\boldsymbol{z}(s),\boldsymbol{z}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)

(4.44)

\displaystyle\quad-\beta\big(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)+\boldsymbol{z}(s)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)-\mu\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}-\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\bigg\}\/\mathrm{d}\/s.

Using (
4
) and (
4.29
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{y}_{0}-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}

\displaystyle=\|\varphi(k,\theta_{-k}\omega,\boldsymbol{y}_{-k})-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}=\|\boldsymbol{v}\big(0,-k;\omega,\boldsymbol{y}_{-k}-\boldsymbol{z}(-k)\big)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{y}_{-k}-\boldsymbol{z}(-k)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\ e^{-\alpha k}+2\int_{-k}^{0}e^{\alpha s}\bigg\{\big((\eta-\alpha)\boldsymbol{z}(s)+\boldsymbol{f},\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)-b\big(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s),\boldsymbol{v}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)

\displaystyle\quad-b\big(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s),\boldsymbol{z}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)-b\big(\boldsymbol{z}(s),\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)-b\big(\boldsymbol{z}(s),\boldsymbol{z}(s),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)

(4.45)

\displaystyle\quad-\beta\big(\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)+\boldsymbol{z}(s)),\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\big)-\mu\|\mathrm{A}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}-\frac{\alpha}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(s)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\bigg\}\/\mathrm{d}\/s.

After combining (
4
) with (
4
), we find

\displaystyle\limsup_{n^{(k)}\to\infty}\|\varphi(t_{n^{(k)}},\theta_{-t_{n^{(k)}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{n^{(k)}})-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\infty}^{-k}h(s)\ \/\mathrm{d}\/s+\|\boldsymbol{y}_{0}-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}-\|\boldsymbol{y}_{-k}-\boldsymbol{z}(-k)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\ e^{-\alpha k}

(4.46)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\infty}^{-k}h(s)\ \/\mathrm{d}\/s+\|\boldsymbol{y}_{0}-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}.

If we define the diagonal process
\{m_{j}\}^{\infty}_{j=1}
by
m_{j}=j^{(j)},j\in\mathbb{N},
the sequence
\{m_{j}\}^{\infty}_{j=k}
is a subsequence of the sequence
(n^{(k)})
and hence by (
4
),

(4.47)

\displaystyle\limsup_{j}\|\varphi(t_{m_{j}},\theta_{-t_{m_{j}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{m_{j}})-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\leq\int_{-\infty}^{-k}h(s)\ \/\mathrm{d}\/s+\|\boldsymbol{y}_{0}-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}.

Taking the limit
k\to\infty
in (
4.47
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\limsup_{j}\|\varphi(t_{m_{j}},\theta_{-t_{m_{j}}}\omega,\boldsymbol{x}_{m_{j}})-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{y}_{0}-\boldsymbol{z}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}},

which proves the claim (
4.25
) and hence the proof of Theorem
4.7
is completed.
∎

From Theorems
4.6
,
4.7
and Theorem 2.8,
[
8
]
, we immediately conclude the main result of this section.

Theorem 4.8
.

Suppose that, for
r\in[1,3],
the domain
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfies Assumption
1.1
and Assumption
2.7
is satisfied. Consider the metric dynamical system,
\Im=(\Omega,\hat{\mathcal{F}},\hat{\mathbb{P}},\hat{\theta})
from Proposition
3.3
, and the RDS
\varphi
on
\mathbb{V}
over
\Im
generated by the 2D SCBF equations (
2.32
) subjected to additive noise satisfying Assumption
2.7
. Then, the family
\mathcal{G}
of sets defined by
\mathcal{G}(\omega)=\Omega_{\mathrm{\bf B}}(\omega),
for all
\omega\in\Omega
, is the minimal
\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{u}
-measurable
\hat{\mathfrak{DK}}
-random attractor for
\varphi
, where
\mathrm{\bf B}
is
\hat{\mathfrak{DK}}
-absorbing set.

5.
Invariant Measures

This section is devoted to show the existence of invariant measures for our model in
\mathbb{V}
. The existence of invariant measures for 2D SCBF equations defined on bounded domains in
\mathbb{H}
is established in
[
39
]
. It is established in
[
21
]
that the existence of compact invariant random set is a sufficient condition for the existence of invariant measures, that is, if a random dynamical system
\varphi
has compact invariant random set, then there exist invariant measures for
\varphi
(Corollary 4.4,
[
21
]
). Since, the random attractor itself is a compact invariant random set, the existence of invariant measures for the 2D SCBF equations (
2.32
) is a direct consequence of Corollary 4.4,
[
21
]
and Theorem
4.8
.

5.1.
Existence of invariant measures

Let us define the transition operator
\{\mathrm{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
by

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{t}f(\boldsymbol{x})=\int_{\Omega}f(\varphi(\omega,t,\boldsymbol{x}))\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[f(\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x}))}}\right],

for all
f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathbb{V})
, where
\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathbb{V})
is the space of all bounded and Borel measurable functions on
\mathbb{V}
and
\varphi
is the random dynamical system corresponding to the 2D SCBF equations (
2.32
), which is defined by (
3.50
). Since
\varphi
is continuous (Theorem
3.8
), Proposition 3.8,
[
11
]
provides the following result:

Lemma 5.1
.

The family
\{\mathrm{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is Feller, that is,
\mathrm{P}_{t}f\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathbb{V})
if
f\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathbb{V})
, where
\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathbb{V})
is the space of all bounded and continuous functions on
\mathbb{V}
. Furthermore, for any
f\in\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathbb{V})
,
\mathrm{P}_{t}f(\boldsymbol{x})\to f(\boldsymbol{x})
as
t\downarrow 0
.

Analogously as in the proof of Theorem 5.6,
[
21
]
, one can prove that
\varphi
is a Markov random dynamical system, that is,
\mathrm{P}_{t_{1}+t_{2}}=\mathrm{P}_{t_{1}}\mathrm{P}_{t_{2}}
, for all
t_{1},t_{2}\geq 0
.
Since, we know by Corollary 4.4,
[
21
]
that if a Markov RDS on a Polish space has an invariant compact random set, then there exists a Feller invariant probability measure
\nu
for
\varphi
.

Definition 5.2
.

A Borel probability measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{V}
is called an
invariant measure
for a Markov semigroup
\{\mathrm{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
of Feller operators on
\mathrm{C}_{b}(\mathbb{V})
if and only if

\mathrm{P}_{t}^{*}\nu=\nu,\ t\geq 0,

where
(\mathrm{P}_{t}^{*}\nu)(\Gamma)=\int_{\mathbb{V}}\mathrm{P}_{t}(\boldsymbol{y},\Gamma)\nu(\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{y}),
for
\Gamma\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{V})
and
\mathrm{P}_{t}(\boldsymbol{y},\cdot)
is the transition probability,
\mathrm{P}_{t}(\boldsymbol{y},\Gamma)=\mathrm{P}_{t}(\chi_{\Gamma})(\boldsymbol{y}),\ \boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{V}
.

By the definition of random attractors, it is clear that there exists an invariant compact random set in
\mathbb{V}
. A Feller invariant probability measure for a Markov RDS
\varphi
on
\mathbb{V}
is, by definition, an invariant probability measure for the semigroup
\{\mathrm{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
defined by (
5.1
). Hence, we have the following result on the existence of invariant measures for the 2D SCBF equations (
2.32
) defined on Poincaré domains in
\mathbb{V}
.

Theorem 5.3
.

There exists an invariant measure for the 2D SCBF equations (
2.32
) in
\mathbb{V}
.

5.2.
Uniqueness of invariant measures

In this work,
\mathrm{W}(t)
is a Wiener process with RKHS
\mathrm{K}
satisfying Assumption
2.7
. In particular,
\mathrm{K}\subset\mathbb{H}
and the natural embedding
i:\mathrm{K}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}
is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator. For a fixed orthonormal basis
\{e_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\mathrm{K}
and a sequence
\{\beta_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of independent Brownian motions defined on some filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},(\mathscr{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}},\mathbb{P})
such that
\mathrm{W}(t)
can be written in the following form

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\beta_{k}(t)e_{k},\ \ \ t\geq 0.

Moreover, there exists a covariance operator
\mathrm{J}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{H})
associated with
\mathrm{W}(t)
defined by

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathrm{J}h_{1},h_{2}}}\right\rangle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle h_{1},\mathrm{W}(1)}}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathrm{W}(1),h_{2}}}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}}}\right],\ \ \ h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathbb{H}.

It is well known from
[
23
]
that
\mathrm{J}
is a non-negative self-adjoint and trace class operator in
\mathbb{H}
. Furthermore,
\mathrm{J}=ii^{*}
and
K=R(\mathrm{J}^{1/2}),
where
R(\mathrm{J}^{1/2})
is the range of the operator
\mathrm{J}^{1/2}
(see
[
7
]
). Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\|ie_{k}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}=\text{Tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{J}}}\right]<\infty.

5.2.1.
Exponential estimates

Here, we obtain exponential estimates which is used to get the uniqueness of invariant measures.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\cdot)
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{2}(\cdot)
be two solutions of the system (
2.32
) with the initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathbb{H}
, respectively. Then, we have

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}_{2}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}}}\right]\leq\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{0}-\boldsymbol{u}_{2}^{0}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\ \emph{\text{exp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{2}{\mu^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{0}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2}{\mu^{2}\alpha}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{2}{\mu^{2}}\emph{\text{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{J}}}\right]-\mu\lambda_{1}-2\alpha}}\right)t}}\right\},

provided
\frac{2}{\mu^{2}}\leq\frac{\alpha}{2\|i^{*}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{H},\mathrm{K})}}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{X}(\cdot)=\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\cdot)-\boldsymbol{u}_{2}(\cdot)
, then
\mathfrak{X}(\cdot)
satisfies the following equality:

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{X}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle=\|\mathfrak{X}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}-2\mu\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathfrak{X}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta-2\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathfrak{X}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta

\displaystyle\quad-2\int_{0}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\zeta))-\mathrm{B}(\boldsymbol{u}_{2}(\zeta)),\mathfrak{X}(\zeta)}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta-2\beta\int_{0}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\zeta))-\mathcal{C}(\boldsymbol{u}_{2}(\zeta)),\mathfrak{X}(\zeta)}}\right\rangle\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathfrak{X}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}-2\mu\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathfrak{X}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta-2\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathfrak{X}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta+2\int_{0}^{t}b(\mathfrak{X}(\zeta),\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\zeta),\mathfrak{X}(\zeta))\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathfrak{X}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}-\mu\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathfrak{X}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta-2\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathfrak{X}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta+\frac{2}{\mu}\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\|\mathfrak{X}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathfrak{X}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}-\int_{0}^{t}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\mu\lambda_{1}+2\alpha)-\frac{2}{\mu}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}}}\right]\|\mathfrak{X}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta,

for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, where we have used (
1.1
), (
2.2
)-(
2.3
), (
2.26
), Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities. The above estimate implies (using Gronwall’s inequality) that

(5.3)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{X}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\leq\|\mathfrak{X}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\ \text{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-(\mu\lambda_{1}+2\alpha)t+\frac{2}{\mu}\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta}}\right).

Let
\mathcal{Z}(t)=\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\mu\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta
, then

\displaystyle\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathcal{Z}

\displaystyle=-\mu\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t-2\alpha\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t-2\beta\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t)\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t),\boldsymbol{f}}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\quad+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t),\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathrm{W}(t)}}\right)+\text{Tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{J}}}\right]\/\mathrm{d}\/t.

Applying Itô’s formula to
\mathfrak{Z}=\text{exp}(\varepsilon\mathcal{Z})
, we have

\displaystyle\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathfrak{Z}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\mathcal{Z}\bigg(-\mu\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t-2\alpha\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t-2\beta\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t)\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t),\boldsymbol{f}}}\right)\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t),\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathrm{W}(t)}}\right)+\text{Tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{J}}}\right]\/\mathrm{d}\/t+2\varepsilon\|i^{*}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\mathcal{Z}\bigg(-\mu\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\alpha-2\varepsilon\|i^{*}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{H},\mathrm{K})}}}\right]\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t-2\beta\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t)\|^{r+1}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{r+1}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}\/\mathrm{d}\/t+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t),\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathrm{W}(t)}}\right)+\text{Tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{J}}}\right]\/\mathrm{d}\/t\bigg).

Choose
\varepsilon>0
such that
2\varepsilon\|i^{*}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{H},\mathrm{K})}\leq\alpha,
then we get

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Z}(t)\leq\text{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varepsilon\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}}}\right)+2\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{Z}(\zeta)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\zeta),\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathrm{W}(\zeta)}}\right)+\varepsilon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\text{Tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{J}}}\right]}}\right)\int_{0}^{t}\mathfrak{Z}(\zeta)\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta.

Taking the expectation and using Gronwall’s inequality, we obtain

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\text{exp}\bigg(\varepsilon\bigg(\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(t)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\mu\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\zeta)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\zeta\bigg)\bigg)\bigg]\leq\text{exp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varepsilon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(0)\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{t}{\alpha}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+t\ \text{Tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{J}}}\right]}}\right)}}\right).

Therefore, if
\frac{2}{\mu^{2}}\leq\frac{\alpha}{2\|i^{*}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{H},\mathrm{K})}},
we obtain (
5.2
) from (
5.3
)-(
5.4
).
∎

Theorem 5.5
.

Let the condition given in Theorem
5.4
be satisfied and
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{0}\in\mathbb{H}
be given. Then, for the condition

\frac{2}{\mu^{2}\alpha}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\frac{2}{\mu^{2}}\emph{\text{Tr}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{J}}}\right]\leq\mu\lambda_{1}+2\alpha,

there is a unique invariant measure to system (
2.32
). Moreover, the invariant measure is ergodic and strongly mixing.

Proof.

See the proof of Theorem 5.5,
[
46
]
.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

Theorem
5.5
establishes the unique invariant measure in
\mathbb{H}
. As
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{H}
is a closed subspace, the uniqueness of invariant measure in
\mathbb{V}
also follows from Theorem
5.5
. The existence and uniqueness of invariant measure in
\mathbb{H}
for 2D SCBF equations (with
r\in[1,3]
) perturbed by a white noise (non degenerate) is obtained in
[
37
]
. The uniqueness of invariant measure in
\mathbb{H}
for 2D as well as 3D SCBF equations driven by additive as well as multiplicative degenerate noise via the asymptotic coupling method is established in
[
48
]
.

Remark 5.7
.

Note that, in the proof of Theorem
5.4
, even though we are using (
1.1
), one can prove without using (
1.1
) as
\alpha>0
.

Remark 5.8
.

For 2D SCBF equations (
1.2
), the results of sections
4
-
5
can be proved in general unbounded domains also. In that case, one has to take the norm defined on
\mathbb{V}
space as
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{V}}:=\|\boldsymbol{u}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}
. Since the Stokes operator
\mathrm{A}
is not invertible in general unbounded domains, one has to make a change in Assumption
2.7
also. Instead of (
2.33
), we need to assume the following: the map

(5.5)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{A})^{-\delta}:\mathrm{K}\to\mathbb{V}\cap\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathcal{O})\ \text{ is }\ \gamma\text{-radonifying,}

for some
\delta\in(0,1/2)
. Under the above change in Assumption
2.7
(which help us to prove Lemma
3.1
in general unbounded domains) and with some minor changes in calculations, all the results of this work hold true in general unbounded domains also.

6.
Approximation of Attractors from Bounded to Unbounded Domain

In this section, we prove the upper semicontinuity of the random attractor, when the domain changes from bounded to unbounded. This upper semicontinuity of the random attractor is entirely different from the upper semicontinuity results established in the work
[
39
]
. For deterministic NSE and CBF equations, upper semicontinuity of global attractors with respect to domain is obtained in
[
59
]
and
[
47
]
, respectively. In order to prove the results of this section, we are extending the theory used for the deterministic model in
[
47
,
59
]
to the stochastic CBF model.

Let
\mathcal{O}=:\mathcal{O}_{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be an unbounded Poincaré domain (for example, one can take
\mathcal{O}=\mathbb{R}\times(-L,L)
). Also let

\mathcal{O}_{m}=\{x\in\mathcal{O}:|x|\leq m\},\ m\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}:=\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}.

It is clear that
\mathcal{O}_{m}\subset\mathcal{O}_{m+1}\cdots\subset\mathcal{O}
, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Due to some technical difficulties (related to the RKHS of Wiener process), we are not able to prove the upper semicontinuity of random attractors with respect to domain for the system (
1.2
). But, we are able to prove if noise in (
1.2
) replaced by a finite dimensional noise (see (
6.1
) below). Consider the following 2D SCBF equations perturbed by additive noise on
\mathcal{O}
:

(6.1)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{u}(t)&=[\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(t)-(\boldsymbol{u}(t)\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\alpha\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\beta|\boldsymbol{u}(t)|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{u}(t)\\
&\qquad-\nabla p(t)+\boldsymbol{f}]\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathcal{W}(t),\text{ in }\ \mathcal{O}\times(0,\infty),\\
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}&=0,\ \text{ in }\ \mathcal{O}\times(0,\infty),\\
\boldsymbol{u}&=\boldsymbol{0},\ \text{ on }\ \partial\mathcal{O}\times(0,\infty),\\
\boldsymbol{u}(0)&=\boldsymbol{x},\ \text{ in }\ \mathcal{O},\end{aligned}}}\right.

with
r\geq 1
and
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}(t,\omega)
is an one-dimensional two-sided Wiener process defined on a probability space
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathscr{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
. Here
\tilde{\Omega}
is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{\Omega}=\{\omega\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}):\omega(0)=0\},

\tilde{\mathscr{F}}
is the Borel sigma-algebra induced by the compact-open topology of
\tilde{\Omega}
, and
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
is the two-sided Gaussian measure on
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathscr{F}})
. Consider the following 2D SCBF equations perturbed by additive noise on
\mathcal{O}_{m}
(
m\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}=:\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
):

(6.2)

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{u}_{m}(t)&=[\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{m}(t)-(\boldsymbol{u}_{m}(t)\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}_{m}(t)-\alpha\boldsymbol{u}_{m}(t)-\beta|\boldsymbol{u}_{m}(t)|^{r-1}\boldsymbol{u}_{m}(t)\\
&\qquad-\nabla p_{m}(t)+\boldsymbol{f}_{m}]\/\mathrm{d}\/t+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{m}\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathcal{W}(t),\text{ in }\ \mathcal{O}_{m}\times(0,\infty),\\
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{m}&=0,\ \text{ in }\ \mathcal{O}_{m}\times(0,\infty),\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{m}&=\boldsymbol{0},\ \text{ on }\ \partial\mathcal{O}_{m}\times(0,\infty),\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{m}(0)&=\boldsymbol{x}_{m},\ \text{ in }\ \mathcal{O}_{m},\end{aligned}}}\right.

where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{f}_{m}(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{f}(x),\ &x\in\mathcal{O}_{m},\\
\boldsymbol{0},\ &x\in\mathcal{O}\backslash\mathcal{O}_{m},\end{cases}\ \ \ \boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{m}(x)=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}(x),\ &x\in\mathcal{O}_{m},\\
\boldsymbol{0},\ &x\in\mathcal{O}\backslash\mathcal{O}_{m},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}_{m}(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{x}(x),\ &x\in\mathcal{O}_{m},\\
\boldsymbol{0},\ &x\in\mathcal{O}\backslash\mathcal{O}_{m}.\end{cases}

Consider, for some
\ell>0

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}(\theta_{t}\omega)=\int_{-\infty}^{t}e^{-\ell(t-s)}\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathcal{W}(s),\ \ \omega\in\tilde{\Omega},

which is the stationary solution of the one dimensional Ornstein-Uhlenbeck equation

\displaystyle\/\mathrm{d}\/\boldsymbol{y}(\theta_{t}\omega)+\ell\boldsymbol{y}(\theta_{t}\omega)\/\mathrm{d}\/t=\/\mathrm{d}\/\mathcal{W}(t).

Let us recall from
[
25
]
that there exists a
\theta
-invariant subset of
\tilde{\Omega}
(will be denoted by
\tilde{\Omega}
itself) of full measure such that
\boldsymbol{y}(\theta_{t}\omega)
is continuous in
t
for every
\omega\in\tilde{\Omega},
and

(6.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\frac{|\boldsymbol{y}(\theta_{t}\omega)|}{|t|}=0\text{\ \ and \ \ }\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{y}(\theta_{s}\omega)\/\mathrm{d}\/s=0.

Moreover,

(6.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-\delta t}|\boldsymbol{y}(\theta_{-t}\omega)|

\displaystyle=0,\ \text{ for all }\ \delta>0.

Assume that
D=\{D(\omega):\omega\in\tilde{\Omega}\}
is a family of non-empty subsets of
E
satisfying, for every
c>0
and
\omega\in\tilde{\Omega}
,

(6.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-ct}\|D(\theta_{-t}\omega)\|_{E}=0,

where
\|D\|_{E}=\sup\limits_{\boldsymbol{x}\in D}\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{E}.
Let
\mathfrak{D}
be the collection of all tempered families of bounded non-empty subsets of
E
, that is,

(6.6)
